TL;DR: Qwen-VLA is a unified embodied foundation model that extends vision-language modeling to continuous action generation via a DiT-based action decoder, enabling heterogeneous tasks (manipulation, navigation, trajectory prediction) across diverse robot embodiments through embodiment-aware prompt conditioning and a unified action-trajectory framework, achieving strong multi-task performance and out-of-distribution generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied intelligence research is fragmented, with specialized models for individual tasks (e.g., manipulation or navigation) that lack generalization across tasks, environments, and robot embodiments. The authors aim to unify heterogeneous embodied decision-making problems within a single vision-language-action model.

Method: Qwen-VLA extends Qwen's vision-language stack with a DiT-based action decoder for continuous action/trajectory generation. It is trained via large-scale joint pretraining on diverse data (robotics manipulation, human egocentric demos, synthetic data, VLN data, trajectory supervision, auxiliary VL data). Embodiment-aware prompt conditioning uses robot-specific text descriptions to specify embodiment and control convention. Manipulation, navigation, and trajectory prediction are cast into a unified action-and-trajectory prediction framework.

Result: Qwen-VLA-Instruct achieves: 97.9% on LIBERO, 73.7% on Simpler-WidowX, 86.1%/87.2% on RoboTwin-Easy/Hard, 69.0% OSR on R2R, 59.6% SR on RxR, 76.9% average OOD success in real-world ALOHA, and 26.6% zero-shot success on DOMINO dynamic manipulation.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous embodied decision-making (manipulation, navigation, trajectory prediction) can be unified within a single vision-language-action model that generalizes across robot morphologies, task families, environments, and out-of-distribution scenarios.

Abstract: Embodied intelligence is often studied through specialized models for individual tasks such as manipulation or navigation, resulting in fragmented capabilities and limited generalization across tasks, environments, and robot embodiments. In this work, we study whether heterogeneous embodied decision-making problems can be unified within a single vision-language-action model. We present Qwen-VLA, a unified embodied foundation model that extends Qwen's vision-language modeling stack from perception, understanding, and reasoning to continuous action and trajectory generation through a DiT-based action decoder. Qwen-VLA is trained with a large-scale joint pretraining recipe over diverse data sources, including robotics manipulation trajectories, human egocentric demonstrations, synthetic simulation data, vision-and-language navigation data, trajectory-centric supervision, and auxiliary vision-language data. To support multiple robot platforms, we introduce embodiment-aware prompt conditioning, where robot-specific textual descriptions specify the current embodiment and control convention. We further cast manipulation, navigation, and trajectory prediction into a unified action-and-trajectory prediction framework, enabling transferable visual grounding, spatial reasoning, and continuous action generation across robot morphologies, task families, and environments. Experiments on manipulation, navigation, and trajectory-centric benchmarks show consistent multi-task performance and out-of-distribution generalization under variations in scene layout, background, lighting, object configuration, and robot embodiment. Qwen-VLA-Instruct achieves 97.9% on LIBERO, 73.7% on Simpler-WidowX, 86.1%/87.2% on RoboTwin-Easy/Hard, 69.0% OSR on R2R, 59.6% SR on RxR, 76.9% average OOD success in real-world ALOHA experiments, and 26.6% zero-shot success on DOMINO dynamic manipulation.

</details>


### [28] [Gaze2Act: Gaze-Conditioned Vision-Language-Action Policies for Interactive Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30282)
*Kuangji Zuo,Gen Li,Bofan Lyu,Yanshuo Lu,Boyu Ma,Shijia Han,Xinyu Zhou,Xichen Yuan,Chuhao Zhou,Jiaqi Bai,Geng Li,Jianfei Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Gaze2Act leverages human gaze as an intuitive intent signal for VLA-based robot manipulation, using cross-view semantic mapping and coarse-to-fine target specification to achieve state-of-the-art performance in intent accuracy and task success on a Unitree G1 humanoid across 16 real-robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language alone is insufficient to precisely convey human intent for robot manipulation, especially for object disambiguation, fine-grained interaction, and dynamic intent changes during execution. Human gaze offers a natural, low-burden, and expressive modality to address this limitation.

Method: Gaze2Act bridges the ego-exo view gap via cross-view semantic matching to map first-person gaze into the robot's perspective, producing an object mask and gaze point for coarse-to-fine target specification. These cues are integrated through perception-level prompting and action-level conditioning for precise interactive manipulation.

Result: Across seven task categories and 16 real-robot tasks on a Unitree G1 humanoid, Gaze2Act achieves state-of-the-art performance in both intent accuracy and task success rate, outperforming baselines in object disambiguation, fine-grained interaction, and dynamic intent steering.

Conclusion: Human gaze provides a natural, low-burden, and highly expressive modality for human-in-the-loop VLA control, effectively complementing language instructions for complex interactive manipulation.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently shown strong potential for robot learning by following language instructions. However, in practice, language alone is often insufficient to precisely convey human intent. It is difficult to describe which exact object to interact with among similar candidates, where to act on the object, or how the target may change during execution. To address this limitation, we propose Gaze2Act, a novel VLA framework that leverages human gaze as a dynamic and intuitive intent signal for complex interactive manipulation. Gaze2Act first bridges the ego-exo view gap by mapping first-person gaze into the robot's perspective through cross-view semantic matching, producing both an object mask and a gaze point for coarse-to-fine target specification. These cues are then integrated into the policy through perception-level prompting and action-level conditioning, allowing the robot to attend to relevant regions and execute precise interactions under dynamic intent. In a systematic evaluation across seven task categories and 16 real-robot tasks on a Unitree G1 humanoid, Gaze2Act achieves state-of-the-art performance in both intent accuracy and task success rate. It notably outperforms baselines in object disambiguation, fine-grained interaction, and dynamic intent steering. These results demonstrate that human gaze provides a natural, low-burden, and highly expressive modality for human-in-the-loop VLA control.

</details>


### [29] [A Heterogeneous Architecture for Robot RL Beyond GPU-Dominant Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30313)
*Yufei Jia,Zhanxiang Cao,Mingrui Yu,Heng Zhang,Shenyu Chen,Dixuan Jiang,Meng Li,Xiaofan Li,Yiyang Liu,Junzhe Wu,Zheng Li,XiLin Fang,Tingyu Cui,Shengcheng Fu,Haoyang Li,Anqi Wang,Zifan Wang,Dongjie Zhu,Chenyu Cao,Zhenbiao Huang,Ziang Zheng,Jie Lu,Xin Ma,Zhengyang Wei,Xiang Zhao,Tianyue Zhan,Ye He,Yuxiang Chen,Yizhou Jiang,Yue Li,Haizhou Ge,Yuhang Dong,Fan Jia,Ziheng Zhang,Meng Zhang,Xiwa Deng,Zhixing Chen,Hanyang Shao,Chenxin Dong,Yixuan Li,Yizhi Chen,Bokui Chen,Kaifeng Zhang,Hanqing Cui,Yusen Qin,Ruqi Huang,Lei Han,Tiancai Wang,Xiang Li,Yue Gao,Guyue Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniLab is a heterogeneous CPU-simulation / GPU-learning architecture for robot RL that decouples CPU-parallel simulation from GPU policy updates via a unified runtime, achieving 3-10× training efficiency gains while supporting cross-platform execution (macOS, AMD ROCm, Intel XPU) and reducing CUDA dependency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the default assumption that efficient robot RL training requires GPU-resident physics simulation, arguing that the critical factor is end-to-end loop efficiency (throughput, learning, synchronization) rather than which processor runs physics.

Method: UniLab uses a heterogeneous architecture with CPU-batched physics backends (MuJoCoUni, MotrixSim) and GPU-based policy learning, connected by a unified runtime that handles data movement, buffering, and synchronization. It supports PPO, SAC, FlashSAC, TD3, and APPO algorithms.

Result: 3-10× improvement in end-to-end training efficiency under same hardware configuration; reduced dependence on NVIDIA CUDA stack; cross-platform support on Apple macOS, AMD ROCm, and Intel XPU backends.

Conclusion: GPU simulation is an effective but not necessary path to efficient robot RL training; CPU-simulation / GPU-learning heterogeneous architectures broaden practical system choices.

Abstract: Simulation-based RL for contemporary robot control is increasingly organized around GPU-resident simulation: physics, rollout collection, and learning are placed on a single GPU-centric execution path. This paradigm has greatly improved training speed, but it has also encouraged a default assumption that efficient training requires physics to reside on the GPU. We revisit this assumption. Our view is that, in simulation-dominated robot control, the essential question is not which processor runs physics, but whether simulation throughput, policy learning, and runtime synchronization form an efficient end-to-end loop. We present UniLab, a heterogeneous CPU-simulation / GPU-learning architecture that decouples CPU-parallel simulation from GPU policy updates through a unified runtime for data movement, buffering, and synchronization. UniLab is implemented as a complete and extensible training system using MuJoCoUni and MotrixSim CPU-batched physics backends, supporting PPO, SAC, FlashSAC, TD3, and APPO. On representative simulation-based robot control tasks, UniLab improves end-to-end training efficiency by 3--10$\times$ under the same hardware configuration, while reducing dependence on the NVIDIA CUDA-based software stack and supporting cross-platform execution on the Apple macOS platform and the AMD ROCm and Intel XPU accelerator backends. These results show that GPU simulation is an effective path to efficient training, but not a necessary one, broadening the practical system choices available for robot RL training. Project page: https://github.com/unilabsim/UniLab.

</details>


### [30] [RoboWits: Unexpected Challenges for Robotic Creative Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30326)
*Chunru Lin,Hongxin Zhang,Fenghao Yu,Zhehuan Chen,Thomas L. Griffiths,Yejin Choi,David Held,Chuang Gan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboWits is a bi-manual robotic benchmark that systematically evaluates cognitive reasoning, creative tool use, and robustness via an automated task generation pipeline with 208 mutation-graded tasks; experiments reveal pre-trained VLAs fail on mutated tasks despite seed-task success, highlighting brittleness in reasoning-demanding manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current robotic benchmarks focus on skill-level execution and lack systematic evaluation of cognitive reasoning, creative problem-solving, and robustness under unexpected conditions.

Method: A multi-agent cooperative framework for automated task generation including seed task generation, verification, metric generation, scene generation, and task mutation; 30 seed tasks expanded to 208 mutated tasks across geometry, material, and assembly-based reasoning dimensions.

Result: Pre-trained VLAs show preliminary success on seed tasks after single-task fine-tuning but struggle significantly on mutated tasks, indicating brittleness in reasoning, strategy adaptation, and robustness under deceptive/constrained environments.

Conclusion: There is a significant performance gap between existing robot policies and the cognitive reasoning demands of real-world manipulation, underscoring the need for benchmarks that go beyond skill execution.

Abstract: The ability to reason, adapt, and creatively solve problems under unexpected challenges is essential for robots operating in real-world environments. However, current robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize skill-level execution and provide limited insight into such cognitive reasoning capabilities. We introduce RoboWits, a bi-manual robotic benchmark designed to systematically evaluate cognitive reasoning, creative tool use, and robustness to unexpected conditions. To enable scalable construction of high-quality reasoning-centric unexpected scenarios, we propose an automated task generation pipeline formulated as a multi-agent cooperative framework, comprising agents for seed task generation and verification, metric generation, scene generation, and task mutation. Using the pipeline, we curated 30 diverse seed tasks and 208 tasks with mutations and graded difficulty across geometry, material, and assembly-based reasoning. We benchmark popular robot policies, pre-trained VLAs, and oracle-state planners. Our results reveal a significant performance gap: while pre-trained VLAs exhibit preliminary success on seed tasks after single-task fine-tuning, they struggle to perform on mutated tasks, implying their brittleness in manipulation tasks requiring reasoning, strategy adaptation, and robustness to deceptive or constrained environments. Project page is available at https://umass-embodied-agi.github.io/RoboWits.

</details>


### [31] [DynaFLIP: Rethinking Robotics Perception via Tri-Modal-Dynamics Guided Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30350)
*Jusuk Lee,Seungjae Lee,Jonghun Shin,Hoseong Jung,Sungha Kim,Daesol Cho,H. Jin Kim,Jia-Bin Huang,Furong Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DynaFLIP is a dynamics-aware multimodal pre-training framework that aligns image, language, and 3D flow modalities via simplex-volume minimization with regularization, producing reusable visual backbones that improve robot manipulation, especially under out-of-distribution scenarios (up to +22.5% gains).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current robot learning pipelines rely on visual encoders pre-trained for static recognition or vision-language alignment, which fail to capture action-relevant dynamics and motion understanding, forcing downstream policies to learn motion from scratch.

Method: DynaFLIP constructs image-language-3D flow triplets from heterogeneous human and robot videos, and trains an image-only encoder by minimizing the simplex volume spanned by the three modalities in a shared hyperspherical space, combined with a cosine regularizer and a contrastive objective to avoid collapse.

Result: DynaFLIP consistently outperforms baselines across diverse simulation and real-world setups and multiple downstream policies (including VLAs), achieving up to +22.5% improvement in out-of-distribution scenarios. The representations focus on control-relevant regions.

Conclusion: Robot generalization improves when visual representations are explicitly trained to encode how the world changes under action, not just what objects are present, demonstrating that pushing motion understanding upstream into perception is beneficial.

Abstract: Robot manipulation critically depends on perception that preserves the action-relevant aspects of a scene. Yet most robot learning pipelines are built upon visual encoders pre-trained for static recognition or vision-language alignment, leaving motion understanding to downstream policies. We introduce DynaFLIP, a dynamics-aware multimodal pre-training framework that pushes motion understanding upstream into perception. We construct image-language-3D flow triplets from heterogeneous human and robot videos, and use these triplets as training-time supervision to shape an image-only encoder. Our key idea is to encourage the three modalities to span a small simplex volume in the shared hyperspherical space -- a smaller simplex volume indicating stronger alignment. To avoid the geometric ambiguity and trivial collapse of naive volume minimization, we combine simplex-volume minimization with a cosine regularizer and a contrastive objective. Our analyses show that DynaFLIP focuses on control-relevant regions critical for manipulation. The resulting dynamics-aware representations serve as reusable visual backbones and consistently outperform baselines across diverse downstream policies, including VLAs. We validate this across diverse simulation and real-world setups, with gains reaching +22.5% under out-of-distribution scenarios. Our results suggest that robot generalization improves when visual representations are trained to encode not just what is present, but how the world changes under action.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [32] [Behavior-Induced Mirror-Prox Temporal-Difference Learning for Faster Off-Policy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28849)
*Xingguo Chen,Yuchen Shen,Shangdong Yang,Chao Li,Guang Yang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes STHTD-MP, a behavior-induced Mirror-Prox temporal-difference method that replaces the feature covariance metric with the symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix, providing theoretical convergence guarantees and showing improved contraction compared to GTD2-MP when the behavior-induced metric enhances saddle-point geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Mirror-Prox TD methods use the feature covariance metric, but hybrid TD methods suggest that behavior-policy transition information can provide a more informative update geometry. The paper aims to leverage this insight for improved off-policy prediction.

Method: STHTD-MP replaces the covariance metric in the primal-dual saddle-point formulation with the symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix, uses a single learning rate for primal and auxiliary variables, and applies a Mirror-Prox prediction-correction step to the resulting hybrid saddle-point operator.

Result: Formal convergence analysis under standard stochastic approximation assumptions; projected-oracle ergodic gap bounds; exact mean-operator comparison with GTD2-MP via spectral radius analysis; numerical benchmarks (two-state, Random Walk, Boyan Chain) support the condition; Baird's counterexample is a singular boundary case where assumptions fail.

Conclusion: STHTD-MP can achieve a smaller mean contraction factor than GTD2-MP when the behavior-induced metric improves the saddle-point geometry, offering a principled way to incorporate transition information for better off-policy TD learning.

Abstract: Gradient temporal-difference methods provide stable off-policy prediction with linear function approximation, but their practical performance is strongly affected by the geometry induced by the auxiliary-variable metric. Existing Mirror-Prox TD methods typically use the feature covariance metric, whereas hybrid TD methods suggest that behavior-policy transition information can provide a more informative update geometry. This paper proposes a behavior-induced Mirror-Prox temporal-difference method, called STHTD-MP, which replaces the covariance metric in the primal-dual saddle-point formulation with the symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix. The method keeps a single learning rate for the primal and auxiliary variables and applies a Mirror-Prox prediction-correction step to the resulting hybrid saddle-point operator. We provide a formal convergence analysis for fixed-policy linear prediction under standard stochastic approximation assumptions: the behavior-induced metric is positive definite, the joint mean system is Hurwitz, boundedness follows from a Lyapunov argument, and the stochastic recursion converges by the ODE method. We further derive projected-oracle ergodic gap bounds and an exact mean-operator comparison with GTD2-MP based on the spectral radius of the deterministic Mirror-Prox error matrix. The analysis shows that STHTD-MP can have a smaller mean contraction factor than GTD2-MP when the behavior-induced metric improves the saddle-point geometry. Exact numerical mean-operator analysis on two-state, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain benchmarks supports this condition, while Baird's counterexample is identified as a singular boundary case where the strict assumptions fail.

</details>


### [33] [Behavior-Aware Auxiliary Corrections for Off-Policy Temporal-Difference Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28855)
*Xingguo Chen,Zhiang He,Yuchen Shen,Shangdong Yang,Chao Li,Guang Yang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies behavior-aware replacements of the auxiliary covariance matrix in TDC/TDRC algorithms for off-policy TD learning with function approximation. It proposes BA-TDC and BA-TDRC, provides theoretical convergence analysis, and empirically evaluates them on standard benchmarks, finding that behavior-aware geometry helps but regularization is needed for robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-policy TD learning with function approximation is unstable, and while TDC/TDRC address this via auxiliary covariance correction, the choice of auxiliary geometry remains underexplored. The authors aim to study behavior-aware replacements of this geometry to better understand its role separate from regularization.

Method: The paper replaces the TDC auxiliary matrix C with the behavior Bellman matrix A_μ to form BA-TDC, then regularizes it to get BA-TDRC. It provides finite-state mean-system formulation, proves fixed-point preservation and almost-sure convergence under Hurwitz stability, and compares deterministic mean rates via spectral radius analysis. Experiments are conducted on two-state counterexample, Baird's counterexample, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain.

Result: Behavior-aware replacement (BA-TDC) is highly beneficial on some tasks but regularization (BA-TDRC) is necessary for robust performance across harder settings. The two-step construction successfully separates the contributions of behavior-aware geometry and regularization.

Conclusion: The auxiliary covariance geometry matters for off-policy TD stability; behavior-aware design can improve performance, but regularization remains crucial for robustness in challenging scenarios.

Abstract: Temporal-difference learning with function approximation can be unstable under off-policy sampling. TDC stabilizes off-policy TD through an auxiliary covariance correction, and TDRC further regularizes this correction in a single-timescale recursion. This paper studies a behavior-aware replacement of the auxiliary covariance geometry in the linear prediction setting, which is the standard local model for understanding the feature-space dynamics of value-function approximation. We first replace the TDC auxiliary matrix (C) by the behavior Bellman matrix (A_μ), yielding BA-TDC, and then regularize the same behavior-aware equation to obtain BA-TDRC. This two-step construction separates the contribution of behavior-aware geometry from the contribution of regularization. The linear analysis also provides a tractable model for an auxiliary-geometry design question that arises in neural-network value approximation, where feature covariances and temporal transition matrices jointly shape the last-layer correction dynamics. We give a finite-state mean-system formulation, prove fixed-point preservation and almost-sure convergence under a Hurwitz stability condition on the instantiated mean system, and compare deterministic mean rates through the spectral radius of the exact linear error recursion. Experiments on the two-state counterexample, Baird's counterexample, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain show that the behavior-aware replacement can be highly beneficial by itself on some tasks, but that regularization is necessary for robust performance across harder settings.

</details>


### [34] [The Cognitive Categorical Transformer: Category-Theoretic Inductive Biases for Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28864)
*Al Kari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Cognitive Categorical Transformer (CCT), a 306M-parameter GPT-2 Small variant augmented with category-theoretic components, achieves 21.27 validation perplexity on WikiText-103 (vs. 24.19 baseline, a 12% reduction). Ablations show 84% of improvement comes from simplicial message passing (GT-Full), while consistency-style categorical priors yield negative results, supporting a structure/consistency distinction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether cognitively grounded, category-theoretic components—particularly simplicial message passing—can improve language model perplexity beyond standard fine-tuning, and to empirically distinguish between structural (topology-adding) and consistency-enforcing categorical priors.

Method: CCT augments a pretrained GPT-2 Small (306M parameters) with components from category theory and cognitive science. It is trained under a matched-step protocol (215K steps, matched data/optimizer/schedule) on WikiText-103. Ablations bypass GT-Full (simplicial message passing) across a seven-phase activation schedule to isolate its contribution. Three negative-result baselines test consistency-style priors (sheaf smoothing, adjunction round-trip, curvature regularization).

Result: CCT achieves 21.27 validation PPL vs. 24.19 for fine-tuned GPT-2 Small (2.92 PPL / 12% relative reduction). Ablation bypassing GT-Full yields 23.72 PPL, localizing 84% (2.45/2.92 PPL) of improvement to simplicial message passing. All three consistency-style priors yield negative results.

Conclusion: First ablation-validated evidence that simplicial message passing improves language-model perplexity at 306M scale. Results support a structure/consistency distinction: categorical priors adding topology help, while those enforcing consistency identities do not.

Abstract: The Cognitive Categorical Transformer (CCT) is a 306M-parameter architecture that augments a pretrained GPT-2 Small backbone with cognitively grounded components derived from category theory and several inspirations from cognitive science. Under a matched-step protocol (215,000 optimizer steps, matched data, matched optimizer and schedule) on WikiText-103, CCT reaches 21.27 validation perplexity, compared with 24.19 for an identically fine-tuned GPT-2 Small baseline. The architecture therefore contributes a 2.92 PPL (12% relative) reduction beyond what in-domain fine-tuning alone provides. A retrain-from-scratch ablation that holds GT-Full simplicial message passing bypassed across the entire seven-phase activation schedule reaches 23.72 PPL, localizing 84% of the architectural improvement (2.45 of 2.92 PPL) to GT-Full. We present the first ablation-validated evidence that simplicial message passing improves language-model perplexity at the 306M-parameter scale on WikiText-103. Published GPT-2 Large reaches 22.05 zero-shot PPL on WikiText-103 with 6.2x more parameters than GPT-2 Small; this paper treats that number as an external published reference, not as the architectural benchmark. Three negative results on consistency-style categorical priors (sheaf smoothing, adjunction round-trip, curvature regularization) and the joint structural-prior result for GT-Full and PrecisionWeightedPP together support an empirical pattern termed the *structure/consistency distinction*, in which categorical priors that add new topology improve language modeling and those that enforce a consistency identity do not.

</details>


### [35] [Ultra-Reduced-Impact-Encased-Logging (URIEL): propose a new method for selective sustainable logging and post-harvest silvicultural treatment in tropical forest using airborne robotics systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28883)
*Daniel Albiero,Gelton Fernando de Morais,Daniela Han,Flávio Roberto de Freitas Gonçalves,Artur Vitório Andrade Santos,Wesllen Lins de Araújo,Alessandra Maia Freire,Cláudio Kiyoshi Umezu,Mateus Peressin,Francesco Toscano,Admilson Írio Ribeiro,Alfeu J. Sguarezi Filho,Américo Ferraz Dias Neto,Angel Pontin Garcia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes URIEL, a novel tropical forest logging method combining heli-logging, robotics, AI, and drone-based silviculture, showing high economic viability and near-elimination of collateral forest damage, though its feasibility depends on integrating multiple stakeholders including high-tech industry, governments, certified companies, and native populations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tropical forests face intense deforestation pressure from economic and political interests, with evidence linking this deforestation to climate change, motivating the need for a more sustainable logging method.

Method: The URIEL method combines heli-logging techniques with robotics and AI, plus post-harvest silvicultural treatments performed by drones. Equipment dimensions were developed, a digital proof of concept was completed, and digital simulations with economic feasibility analysis were conducted for various helicopter-timber-distance combinations.

Result: The URIEL method demonstrated high economic viability and can virtually eliminate collateral damage to forests while maintaining ecosystem services.

Conclusion: Despite satisfactory scientific and technological results, the feasibility of URIEL depends on the integration of key stakeholders: high-tech industry, political governments, certified logging companies, and native populations.

Abstract: Tropical forests worldwide are under intense deforestation pressure driven by economic and political interests, and scientific evidence suggests this deforestation contributes to climate change. This paper proposes a novel logging method for tropical forests, Ultra-Reduced-Impact-Encased-Logging (URIEL). This new method is based on heli-logging techniques combined with intensive use of robotics and AI integrated with post-harvest silvicultural treatments performed by drones. The concept of appropriate equipment for this method was developed, dimensions were determined, details were completed in a digital proof of concept, and an effective digital simulation and economic feasibility analysis were carried out for various helicopter-timber-distance combinations. The results demonstrated that a URIEL method has high economic viability and makes it possible to virtually eliminate collateral damage to forests while maintaining ecosystem services. The main conclusion of this paper is that, despite the satisfactory scientific and technological results, the feasibility of a Uriel method depends on the integration of stakeholders intrinsic to the context: high-tech industry; political governments; certified logging companies; and native populations.

</details>


### [36] [Review Arcade: On the Human Alignment and Gameability of LLM Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28897)
*Hans Ole Hatzel,Sebastian Steindl,Jan Strich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates LLM-generated reviews for scientific papers, finding limited alignment with human reviews, significant variation across prompts/models, and that iterative "gaming" of LLM reviews can inflate scores for up to 35% of papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With LLM-generated reviews being officially piloted by major conferences, it is critical to understand whether LLM reviews align with human judgments and whether the system can be exploited by authors using LLMs to iteratively revise papers based on automated feedback.

Method: Empirical experiments on papers from the 2025 ACL Rolling Review (ARR), comparing LLM-generated reviews against human reviews under varying prompts and models, and testing an iterative draft-revise workflow where authors improve submissions based on LLM review feedback.

Result: LLM-human alignment is limited at best, varies substantially across prompts and models, and iterative revision guided by LLM reviews can lead to statistically significant score increases for up to 35% of papers.

Conclusion: The paper highlights vulnerabilities in LLM-assisted peer review pipelines, showing both limited reliability of LLM reviews and potential for adversarial manipulation by authors.

Abstract: LLM-generated reviews for scientific papers are gaining considerable traction and are even being officially piloted by major conferences. We have to assume that not only reviewers are using LLM-assistance, but also that authors use LLMs to revise their papers before submitting. In this work, we perform empirical experiments on papers from the 2025 ACL Rolling Review (ARR) to evaluate LLM reviews from both the author and the reviewer perspective. First, we identify a limited alignment of LLM reviews with human ones. In the best-case scenario, the alignment is reasonable. However, we also find that LLM-human alignment varies substantially across prompts and models. Finally, we investigate the scenario in which the author uses an iterative draft-revise workflow to improve the submission according to the LLM review. We find that this "gaming" of LLM reviews can be effective in specific scenarios, leading to a statistically significant increase of overall scores for up to 35\% of papers. We publish our code: https://github.com/uhh-hcds/reviewarcade.

</details>


### [37] [Orthogonal Concept Erasure for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28902)
*Yuhao Sun,Lingyun Yu,Haoxiang Xu,Fengyuan Miao,Zhuoer Xu,Hongtao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Orthogonal Concept Erasure (OCE), a method that reformulates editing-based concept erasure in diffusion models as multiplicative orthogonal parameter updates, enabling precise concept removal while preserving overall generative capacity. It achieves superior performance on single- and multi-concept erasure tasks, erasing up to 100 concepts in 4.3 seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concept erasure methods face a trade-off: training-based methods are effective but computationally expensive, while editing-based methods are efficient but fail to simultaneously achieve precise concept erasure and preserve overall generative capacity due to their reliance on additive parameter updates that entangle direction, magnitude, and angular geometry.

Method: OCE reformulates editing-based erasure as multiplicative parameter updates using layer-wise orthogonal transformations derived from a closed-form solution. It preserves neuron magnitude and angular geometry while erasing concept semantics (which primarily depend on neuron direction). For multi-concept erasure, it introduces a subspace-level objective with structured subspace manipulation.

Result: OCE outperforms existing methods in both concept erasure effectiveness and non-target preservation, and can erase up to 100 concepts in 4.3 seconds.

Conclusion: By addressing the core limitation of additive updates in editing-based concept erasure through geometric multiplicative updates, OCE achieves scalable, precise, and efficient concept erasure without compromising generative capacity.

Abstract: Concept erasure has emerged as a promising approach to mitigate undesired or unsafe content in diffusion models, yet existing methods still face significant limitations. While training-based methods are effective, their high computational cost limits scalability. Editing-based methods are more efficient and deployment-friendly, yet they struggle to simultaneously achieve precise concept erasure and preserve overall generative capacity. We identify this core limitation of the editing-based methods as reliance on additive parameter updates. Our empirical analysis reveals that concept semantics primarily depend on neuron direction rather than neuron magnitude, while overall generative capacity relies on the angular geometry of neurons. As additive updates inherently entangle direction, magnitude, and angular geometry, they inevitably introduce unintended interference between concept erasure and overall generation performance. To address this, we propose Orthogonal Concept Erasure (OCE), which reformulates editing-based erasure as multiplicative parameter updates from a geometric perspective. Specifically, OCE applies layer-wise orthogonal transformations derived from a closed-form solution to the parameters, enabling precise concept erasure while preserving the neuron magnitude and angular geometry. Furthermore, to address conflicting constraints in multi-concept erasure, OCE introduces a subspace-level objective with structured subspace manipulation, yielding a more effective and scalable erasure. Extensive experiments on single- and multi-concept erasure demonstrate that OCE outperforms existing methods in concept erasure and non-target preservation, erasing up to 100 concepts in 4.3 s. Code: https://github.com/HansSunY/OCE.

</details>


### [38] [Frontier LLM-based agents can overcome the ontology curation bottleneck for natural phenotypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28965)
*James P. Balhoff,Hilmar Lapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Five frontier LLMs acting as agentic curators matched the inter-curator variability of trained human biocurators in phenotype annotation, substantially outperforming the previous NLP tool (Semantic CharaParser) across all metrics, with the best agents approaching but not exceeding the top human curator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phenotype annotation (linking free-text descriptions to ontology terms) is labor-intensive and relies on highly trained human experts, making it challenging to scale. Previous work showed machine-human consistency was significantly lower than inter-curator consistency, highlighting the need for improved automated approaches.

Method: Five frontier hosted LLMs from Anthropic and OpenAI were deployed as "agentic curators" within a self-contained workspace with the source publication PDF, annotation guide, four project ontologies (UBERON, PATO, BSPO, GO), and a validation script. Their EQ annotations were evaluated against the same Gold Standard used in Dahdul et al. (2018) alongside comparisons to three human curators and Semantic CharaParser.

Result: All five LLM agents fell within the range of inter-curator variability of the three trained human biocurators. The best performing agents approached but did not reach the best performing human curator. Agents substantially outperformed Semantic CharaParser on all four evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: LLM-based agentic curation is a viable approach that can match human-level consistency in phenotype annotation, significantly improving upon prior NLP methods, though the top human curator performance remains the ceiling.

Abstract: Linking free-text phenotype descriptions to ontology terms, typically referred to as phenotype annotation, is essential for the cross-study integration of comparative morphological data. This labor intensive process has heavily relied on highly trained human experts, which makes it challenging to scale and thus a key bottleneck. Dahdul et al. (2018) established a Gold Standard (GS) of Entity-Quality (EQ) annotations across seven phylogenetic studies and used it to evaluate three human curators and the Semantic CharaParser NLP tool with ontology-based semantic similarity metrics; they reported that machine-human consistency was significantly lower than inter-curator (human-human) consistency. Here we revisit that benchmark with five frontier hosted LLMs from Anthropic and OpenAI, each operating as an "agentic curator" within a self-contained workspace that supplies the source publication PDF, the same annotation guide used by the original human curators, the four project ontologies (UBERON, PATO, BSPO, GO), and a validation script. Evaluated against the same Gold Standard, every agent fell within the range of inter-curator variability of the three trained human biocurators of the original study; the best performing agents approached but did not reach the best performing human curator. Agents substantially outperformed Semantic CharaParser on all four metrics.

</details>


### [39] [VFEAgent: A Multimodal Agent Framework for End-to-End Automated Finite Element Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28978)
*Jiachen Zhang,Junyi Lao,Chenghao Liu,Siyuan Liu,Shixin Wu,Linsen Zhang,Boyu Wang,Songfang Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VFEAgent is an end-to-end multi-agent system that automates Finite Element Analysis (FEA) from images and text descriptions, using a multimodal vision-language pipeline and a verification-first code synthesis framework to generate physically valid simulations, outperforming LLM-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FEA workflows are complex and heavily dependent on domain expertise. Existing LLM-based approaches struggle with multimodal inputs and complex task execution, motivating the need for a more comprehensive automation system.

Method: VFEAgent integrates (1) a multimodal vision-language multi-agent pipeline using ReAct-driven reasoning to extract structured FEA specifications from images and text, and (2) a verification-first code synthesis framework with self-debugging and fallback mechanisms to ensure executability and physical validity.

Result: The system achieves high success rates in generating complete and physically valid simulations across various engineering mechanics scenarios, outperforming LLM-based baseline methods in reliability and correctness.

Conclusion: The findings validate the feasibility of fully automating the FEA workflow, demonstrating the framework's potential to free engineers from manual analysis tasks.

Abstract: Finite Element Analysis (FEA) serves as the cornerstone of modern engineering design. However, its workflow is inherently complex and relies heavily on domain expertise. Although recent efforts have integrated Large Language Models (LLMs) into FEA, existing approaches face limitations in handling multimodal inputs and executing complex tasks. To address these limitations, we propose VFEAgent, an end-to-end multi-agent system designed to automate FEA modeling and simulation directly from input images and problem descriptions. Our methodology integrates two core components: (1) a multimodal vision-language multi-agent pipeline that employs ReAct-driven reasoning to extract structured FEA specifications from heterogeneous inputs and (2) a verification-first code synthesis framework, incorporating robust self-debugging and fallback mechanisms to ensure executability and physical validity. We systematically evaluated the system across various engineering mechanics scenarios. The results demonstrate that VFEAgent achieves a high success rate in generating complete and physically valid simulations, outperforming LLM-based baseline methods in reliability and correctness. These findings validate the feasibility of automating the complete FEA workflow, highlighting the framework's potential to liberate engineers from tedious manual analysis.

</details>


### [40] [BEAMS: Benchmarking and Evaluating AI for Modeling and Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28994)
*Sara Metcalf,William Schoenberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The BEAMS Initiative develops benchmarks and open infrastructure to evaluate AI tools for modeling and simulation, focusing on human-centered, responsible practices. Tests across qualitative, quantitative, and discussion tasks show AI tools perform better at discussion and basic qualitative tasks than causal reasoning and error fixing, with no single LLM dominating across all engines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world decision-making AI must build interpretable simulation models that complement human expertise rather than replace it. The BEAMS Initiative aims to guide AI tool development toward responsible, ethical, and human-centered modeling and simulation practices through standardized benchmarks.

Method: The initiative uses open digital and organizational infrastructure, including the open-source sd-ai project, a steering group for prioritizing benchmarks, and a technical group implementing automated tests. Evaluations span categories: causal translation, model iteration, causal reasoning, conformance, model behavior explanation, suggested steps, and suggested fixes. Tests are applied to AI tools supporting qualitative model building, quantitative model building, and model discussion.

Result: AI tools perform better at discussion and basic qualitative tasks than causal reasoning and quantitative error fixing. No single LLM dominates across engine types; performance variability highlights task-specific tradeoffs between speed and accuracy.

Conclusion: The evaluations demonstrate the need for diverse benchmarks. Ongoing work will incorporate benchmarks addressing bias through alternative perspectives and human-centered use cases.

Abstract: AI tools to support real world decision making must be able to build simulation models that inform their recommendations and render them interpretable. Tools that can automate aspects of modeling practice must complement human expertise, not replace it. The BEAMS Initiative aims to guide the development of AI tools for modeling and simulation toward forms that are responsible and ethical by establishing benchmarks for human centered modeling and simulation practices. The initiative uses open digital and organizational infrastructure to collaboratively evaluate AI tools for modeling and simulation. The open source sd ai project hosted by the initiative establishes transparency and enables contributions to be shared broadly. A steering group focuses on prioritizing potential benchmarks, while a technical group focuses on implementing the benchmarks in the form of automated tests. Tests for several distinct categories of evaluation have been implemented and applied to AI tools that support qualitative model building, quantitative model building, and model discussion. These include tests for causal translation, model iteration, causal reasoning, conformance, model behavior explanation, suggested model building steps, and suggested model fixes. When engines from the sd ai project are coupled with different LLMs, their performance on these evaluations reveals variability across different AI tools. The evaluations implemented by the initiative demonstrate that AI enabled modeling tools perform better at discussion and basic qualitative tasks than with causal reasoning and quantitative error fixing. No single LLM dominates across engine types, highlighting the importance of specific tasks and tradeoffs between speed and accuracy. Ongoing efforts of the initiative aim to incorporate benchmarks that address concerns about bias by considering alternative perspectives and human centered use cases.

</details>


### [41] [Adopt $\neq$ Adapt: Longitudinal Analyses of LLM Conversations in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29018)
*Rebecca M. M. Hicke,Kiran Tomlinson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of ~12,000 Bing Copilot users and WildChat-4.8M reveals that individual user-LLM interaction habits are highly stable over time, with significant user heterogeneity, and that WildChat data is skewed toward power users and not representative of typical interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on user-LLM interactions provides only static snapshots; little is known about how individual users' behavior evolves over time.

Method: Analyzed conversational trajectories of ~12,000 randomly sampled Microsoft Bing Copilot users over time and compared with WildChat-4.8M dataset; examined population-level trends, individual user trajectories, activity-level differences, and dataset biases.

Result: Population-level trends exist but individual user trajectories show weak trends; user habits are highly sticky. More active users have more successful conversations and engage in more complex/professional tasks. WildChat-4.8M is significantly skewed toward highly proficient power users.

Conclusion: Existing user behavior is difficult to change; user heterogeneity is substantial. WildChat does not represent typical user-AI interactions, which is an important caveat for downstream uses of the data.

Abstract: Although a growing body of research has begun to describe user--LLM interactions, the picture it paints is largely static; little is known about how individual users change their behavior over time. To address this gap, we analyze the conversational trajectories of $\sim$12,000 randomly sampled Microsoft Bing Copilot users and compare these with data from WildChat-4.8M. While the Copilot data contains significant population-level trends, we find that trends in individual user trajectories are much weaker; user habits prove to be overwhelmingly sticky. We also find stark differences between users of different activity levels: more active users have more successful conversations and use the LLM for more complex and professionally oriented tasks. Some user trends also appear in WildChat-4.8M, but we find evidence that this dataset is significantly skewed towards highly proficient "power" users. Ultimately, our results suggest that existing user behavior is difficult to change and demonstrate the extent of user heterogeneity. Our comparison between datasets highlights that WildChat does not represent typical user-AI interactions, an important caveat for downstream uses of the data.

</details>


### [42] [When Models Disagree: Rethinking LLM Evaluation for Public Comment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29025)
*Aisha Najera,Alvin Moon,Vedant Srinivasan,Rajesh Veeraraghavan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an Interpretive Audit Pipeline for evaluating LLMs used to categorize public comments, finding that multi-model disagreement reveals interpretive complexity better than standard accuracy metrics alone, and that human annotators introduce novel framings not captured by LLM ensembles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation based on stance accuracy against a small validated set fails to detect when different LLMs produce materially different categorizations of the same public input, potentially shaping what policymakers see and which arguments register.

Method: The authors propose an Interpretive Audit Pipeline that treats multi-model disagreement as diagnostic of interpretive complexity. They analyze 1,260 public comments on a federal USDA docket across four LLMs, comparing inter-model divergence vs. within-model prompt variation, and conduct a two-stage labeling study on a stratified 40-comment subsample where four LLMs and a human annotator labeled independently and then revised after seeing others' labels.

Result: Inter-model thematic divergence exceeds within-model prompt variation; an expert rubric suppresses deep interpretive disagreement without resolving it; revision behavior varied across labelers; and the human annotator's revisions frequently introduced framings absent from the ensemble's collective output.

Conclusion: Disagreement-based evaluation is a necessary complement to accuracy metrics for LLM-assisted interpretive coding.

Abstract: Federal agencies are deploying large language models (LLMs) to categorize public comment corpora, where the model's organization of the record shapes what policymakers see and which arguments register. Standard evaluation, anchored on stance accuracy against a small validated set, cannot detect when different models produce materially different categorizations of the same public input. We propose an Interpretive Audit Pipeline that treats multi-model disagreement as diagnostic of interpretive complexity and directs human review toward genuinely ambiguous public input. Analyzing 1,260 public comments on a federal USDA docket across four LLMs, we find that inter-model thematic divergence exceeds within-model prompt variation, and that an expert rubric suppresses deep interpretive disagreement without resolving it. In a two-stage labeling study on a stratified 40-comment subsample, four LLMs and a human annotator labeled independently and then revised after seeing the others' labels. Revision behavior varied across labelers, and the human annotator's revisions frequently introduced framings absent from the ensemble's collective output. We argue disagreement-based evaluation is a necessary complement to accuracy metrics for LLM-assisted interpretive coding.

</details>


### [43] [Mind Your Tone: Does Tone Alter LLM Performance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29027)
*Om Dobariya,Akhil Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study investigates how tonal variations in prompts affect LLM accuracy on multiple-choice questions, finding that tonal effects are systematic but model-dependent, with some models showing significant accuracy swings, and proposes a routing framework to explain tone-induced shifts in internal reasoning modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine whether and how tonal variations in prompts lead to disparate LLM accuracy for objective multiple-choice questions, given that LLM performance is observed to vary based on prompting styles and tones.

Method: Used two datasets: a 50-base question dataset with five tone variants and a 570-base MMLU subset spanning 57 subjects with seven tone variants. Evaluated four LLMs: ChatGPT-4o, ChatGPT-5-nano, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite.

Result: Tonal effects are systematic but highly model-dependent; some models show small statistically significant shifts while others exhibit large accuracy swings; subject-level differences in tone sensitivity were identified.

Conclusion: Users should not assume tone-robust reliability in LLM deployments; a routing framework is presented to explain how tones may attune internal reasoning modes.

Abstract: The use of Large Language Models (LLMs) is proliferating, yet their performance is observed to vary based on prompting styles and tones. In this study, we investigate both whether and how tonal variations in prompts lead to disparate LLM accuracy for objective multiple-choice questions. We use two datasets: a 50-base question dataset with five tone variants and a 570-base question MMLU subset spanning 57 subjects with seven tone variants. Experiments were conducted to evaluate the performance of four cost-efficient, popular LLMs: ChatGPT-4o, ChatGPT-5-nano, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite. Across models, tonal effects are systematic but highly model-dependent. Some models show small, yet statistically significant, shifts, while others exhibit large accuracy swings across tones. Further, we identify subject-level differences in tone sensitivity and present a routing framework to explain how tones may attune internal reasoning modes. Our findings caution users against assuming tone-robust reliability in LLM deployments.

</details>


### [44] [Practitioner Beliefs and Behaviors in AI-Enhanced Education: DOT Framework Survey Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29041)
*David Gibson,M. Elizabeth Azukas,Gerald Knezek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey of 72 higher education practitioners reveals they have favorable views of AI as pedagogical support but emphasize human oversight, with limited design-oriented practices and widespread institutional barriers (policy, training, infrastructure). Exploratory factor analysis identified a three-factor belief structure (AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, Instructor Collaboration) with high reliability (α = .90).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand higher education practitioners' beliefs, behaviors, and institutional conditions regarding AI integration, and to provide empirical support for the DOT Framework (Design Thinking + Open Systems Theory) as a descriptive model.

Method: Cross-sectional survey (n=72) of higher education practitioners; exploratory factor analysis on 19 belief items; grounded in the DOT Framework integrating design thinking and open systems theory.

Result: Three-factor belief structure identified (AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, Instructor Collaboration and Planning; α=.90). Practitioners favor AI for pedagogy but insist on human oversight. Practices emphasize iterative prompting and content generation over needs assessment/feedback loops. Widespread institutional barriers reported (policy, training, infrastructure gaps).

Conclusion: Provides preliminary empirical support for the DOT Framework as a descriptive model of practitioner beliefs and practices. Highlights theory-practice gaps in design-oriented AI implementation. Establishes initial measurement structure and identifies need for confirmatory validation and outcome-based research linking AI-supported design to instructional quality.

Abstract: This study reports findings from a cross-sectional survey (n = 72) of higher education practitioners examining beliefs, behaviors, and institutional conditions related to artificial intelligence (AI) integration in teaching and learning. Grounded in the DOT Framework, which integrates design thinking and open systems theory, the study investigates AI familiarity, usage patterns, design-oriented practices, and pedagogical beliefs. Exploratory factor analysis of 19 belief items identified a three-factor structure: AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, and Instructor Collaboration and Planning (α = .90). Results indicate that practitioners hold favorable views of AI as a pedagogical support while maintaining strong commitments to human oversight and critical evaluation. Reported practices emphasize iterative prompting and content generation, with less consistent use of needs assessment and feedback loops. Institutional barriers including limited policy, training, and infrastructure were widely reported. These findings provide preliminary empirical support for the DOT Framework as a descriptive model of practitioner beliefs and practices, while also highlighting gaps between design-oriented theory and current implementation. The study contributes an initial measurement structure and identifies directions for confirmatory validation and outcome-based research linking AI-supported design practices to instructional quality.

</details>


### [45] [Differentiable Belief-based Opponent Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29042)
*Aarav G Sane,Karthik Sivachandran,Rohan Paleja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: D-BOS is a first-order method that shapes opponents by differentiating through their belief dynamics (k-step softmax-Bayes), allowing optimal strategies to emerge naturally from reward structures, outperforming PPO and BBM in hidden-role games, especially mixed-motive settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human coordination relies on influencing others' beliefs through strategic action, but existing opponent shaping methods operate in parameter/policy/value space, and belief-manipulation techniques in hidden-role games rely on hard-coded objectives like deception or belief saturation.

Method: D-BOS treats each observer's belief as the shaped opponent state, differentiates through k-step softmax-Bayes belief dynamics, and aggregates gradients over multiple observers' inferred belief trajectories for the opponent-shaping signal, without explicit reward engineering for deception or cooperation.

Result: D-BOS outperforms PPO and BBM in hidden-role games, with the largest performance gains observed in mixed-motive settings.

Conclusion: The belief-space formulation provides a natural opponent-shaping signal that allows optimal strategies to emerge from the environment's reward structure, rather than requiring hand-crafted objectives.

Abstract: Human coordination often relies on the ability to influence the beliefs of others through strategic action. In multi-agent reinforcement learning, opponent shaping attempts to replicate this influence, though existing methods typically operate within an opponent's parameter, policy, or value space. Meanwhile, belief-manipulation techniques in hidden-role games often rely on hard-coded objectives, such as deception or belief saturation. We propose Differentiable Belief-based Opponent Shaping (D-BOS), a first-order method that treats each observer's belief as the shaped opponent state and differentiates through $k$-step softmax-Bayes belief dynamics. Rather than explicitly rewarding deceptive or cooperative behavior, our method treats the belief state as the target for shaping. This allows the optimal strategy to emerge naturally from the environment's reward structure. This belief-space formulation provides an opponent-shaping signal by differentiating through opponent belief updates, and naturally extends to multiple observers by aggregating gradients over their individual inferred belief trajectories. Empirically, D-BOS outperforms PPO and BBM in hidden-role games, with the largest gains in mixed-motive settings.

</details>


### [46] [Hallucination Mitigation with Agentic AI, Nested Learning, and AI Sustainability via Semantic Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29055)
*Diego Gosmar,Deborah A. Dahl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a memory-augmented multi-agent architecture (HOPE-inspired Nested Learning with CMS and semantic caching) that reduces hallucination by 31-36% in LLM pipelines, achieving strong mitigation-observability trade-offs without model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucination remains a major reliability barrier for production LLM systems, especially in multi-agent pipelines where unsupported claims can propagate unchecked across stages.

Method: Adapts a HOPE-inspired Nested Learning architecture with Continuum Memory Systems (CMS) and semantic similarity caching; evaluated on a hybrid benchmark of 310 prompts via a three-stage agentic pipeline orchestrated through OFP, with five KPIs aggregated into THS across five weighting configurations.

Result: End-to-end THS reductions of -31.3% to -35.9%; semantic caching achieved 47.3% hit rate (440/930), reducing LLM invocations to 490; ExtremeObservability achieved the most negative final THS (-0.0709).

Conclusion: Memory-augmented multi-agent designs can jointly improve factual reliability, operational efficiency, and auditability without model retraining.

Abstract: Hallucination remains a major reliability barrier for production LLM systems, particularly in multi-agent pipelines where unsupported claims can propagate unchecked across stages. This paper adapts a HOPE-inspired Nested Learning architecture with Continuum Memory Systems (CMS) and semantic similarity caching to a hybrid benchmark of 310 prompts combining 217 epistemic-uncertainty prompts and 93 fabrication-induction stress-test prompts. A three-stage agentic pipeline orchestrated via the Open Floor Protocol (OFP) is evaluated with five KPIs -- FCD (Factual Claim Density), FGR (Factual Grounding References), FDF (Fictional Disclaimer Frequency), ECS (Explicit Contextualization Score), and OSR (Observability Score Ratio) -- aggregated into THS (Total Hallucination Score) across five weighting configurations to study mitigation-observability trade-offs. FDF, ECS, OSR, and FGR are subtracted as mitigation signals, so that a more negative THS indicates stronger mitigation. The FrontEndAgent is configured as a high-stochasticity generator (temperature = 1.0) to produce a realistic hallucination baseline, while the SecondLevelReviewer and ThirdLevelReviewer operate as progressive correctors. This asymmetric design yields end-to-end THS reductions of -31.3% to -35.9% across five weighting configurations. Semantic caching achieves 440 cache hits over 930 potential calls (47.3% hit rate), reducing LLM invocations to 490, lowering energy and CO2e footprint, and making multi-stage review pipelines operationally viable at production scale. ExtremeObservability attains the most negative final THS (-0.0709), confirming that observability-heavy configurations reinforce rather than compromise mitigation. These findings suggest that memory-augmented multi-agent designs can jointly improve factual reliability, operational efficiency, and auditability without model retraining.

</details>


### [47] [Robust and Efficient Guardrails with Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29068)
*Siddharth Sai,Xiaofei Wen,Muhao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COLAGUARD is a guardrail model that compresses multi-step safety reasoning into continuous latent space via stage-wise training, achieving performance matching explicit reasoning baselines (GuardReasoner) while delivering 12.9X speedup and 22.4X fewer tokens, improving macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety guardrails using explicit reasoning (e.g., GuardReasoner) achieve high accuracy but incur prohibitive latency and token overhead, making them impractical for high-throughput deployment. The paper aims to bridge this gap by preserving reasoning quality while drastically improving inference efficiency.

Method: COLAGUARD transfers multi-step safety reasoning into a continuous latent space through a stage-wise training curriculum, enabling direct hidden-state propagation at inference instead of generating explicit rationale tokens.

Result: Evaluated on 10 prompt- and response-moderation settings across 8 safety benchmarks, COLAGUARD improves macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3, matches GuardReasoner in macro-F1, while achieving 12.9X speedup and 22.4X reduction in token usage.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning provides a practical alternative to explicit rationale generation for deployable guardrails, jointly optimizing safety robustness and inference efficiency rather than treating them as competing objectives.

Abstract: Maintaining the safety of large language models (LLMs) is crucial as they are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. Existing safety guardrails typically rely on single-pass classification or, more recently, distilled reasoning. Reasoning-based guardrails significantly outperform classification-only baselines, but they incur substantial query latency and token overhead that make them impractical for highthroughput deployment. To address this challenge, we propose COLAGUARD, a guardrail model that transfers multi-step safety reasoning into a continuous latent space through a stage-wise training curriculum, enabling direct hidden-state propagation at inference. Evaluated on ten prompt- and response-moderation settings spanning eight safety benchmarks, COLAGUARD improves macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3 and matches our explicit reasoning baseline, GuardReasoner, in macroF1 while delivering a 12.9X speedup and 22.4X reduction in token usage. Our results suggest that latent reasoning offers a practical alternative to explicit rationale generation for deployable guardrails, jointly improving safety robustness and inference efficiency rather than treating them as competing objectives.

</details>


### [48] [Bridging the Sim-to-Real Gap in Reinforcement Learning-Based Industrial Dispatching through Execution Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29078)
*Jonathan Hoss,Noah Klarmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A policy-neutral execution and measurement layer is proposed to mediate between event-driven scheduling policies and industrial environments, enabling separation of decision semantics from execution behavior, structured error attribution, and improved reliability under asynchronous and partially observed states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event-driven scheduling policies in industrial environments face issues of temporally inconsistent decision states, undefined action admissibility, and ambiguous execution error origins, which collectively limit reliability and interpretability.

Method: A policy-neutral execution and measurement layer is designed to: (1) construct decision-valid snapshots from asynchronous event streams; (2) define a standardized execution contract with explicit action admissibility; (3) record outcomes as divergences between policy intent, transactional outcomes, physical execution, and human intervention.

Result: Evaluated via discrete-event simulation, the layer shows analytical benefits across all observation lag regimes by transforming undifferentiated execution failures into structured, typed outcomes with full attribution coverage. Operational benefits are strongest under low observation lag, where avoidable execution errors can be prevented before commitment.

Conclusion: The proposed layer turns execution uncertainty into supervisory data for evaluation and policy refinement, enabling separation of decision semantics from execution behavior and making deployment mismatch observable and structurally attributable.

Abstract: Event-driven scheduling policies are increasingly deployed in industrial environments, where decisions are made under asynchronous and partially observed system states. As a result, decision states are not temporally consistent, action admissibility is not explicitly defined, and the origin of execution errors remains ambiguous. These issues limit both reliability and interpretability.
  To address this gap, a policy-neutral execution and measurement layer is proposed to mediate between scheduling policies and the industrial execution environment. The layer constructs decision-valid snapshots from asynchronous event streams, defines a standardized execution contract with explicit action admissibility, and records outcomes as divergences between policy intent, transactional outcomes, physical execution, and human intervention. This enables a separation between decision semantics and execution behavior and makes deployment mismatch observable and structurally attributable.
  The proposed framework is evaluated using a discrete-event simulation. The results show analytical benefits across all observation lag regimes, as undifferentiated execution failures are transformed into structured, typed outcomes with full attribution coverage. Operational benefits are strongest under low observation lag, where avoidable execution errors can be prevented before commitment. Overall, the layer turns execution uncertainty into supervisory data for evaluation and policy refinement.

</details>


### [49] [The Importance of Out-of-Band Metadata for Safe Autonomous Agents: The Redpanda Agentic Data Plane](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29082)
*Tyler Akidau,Tyler Rockwood,Johannes Brüderl,Marc Millstone*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Redpanda Agentic Data Plane (ADP), an architecture that uses out-of-band metadata channels to enforce security governance for AI agents across their entire lifecycle, demonstrated in a multi-agent portfolio rebalancing system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are increasingly acting as digital employees but are simultaneously less predictable (hallucination, misinterpretation, adversarial manipulation) and more technically capable (deep system knowledge, high-throughput interfaces) than humans, making it unsafe to rely on them to faithfully propagate security-critical metadata like access policies and data classifications.

Method: The Redpanda Agentic Data Plane (ADP) architecture uses out-of-band metadata channels — infrastructure pathways that carry security context, policy signals, and audit trails deterministically outside the agent's read/write path and across heterogeneous infrastructure — to enforce governance at every agent lifecycle stage.

Result: Demonstrated with a multi-agent portfolio rebalancing system where autonomous agents monitor markets, make trades, and execute orders across isolated client accounts, with per-client data scoping, trade approval thresholds, and tamper-proof audit trails enforced by out-of-band channels that agents cannot see or bypass.

Conclusion: Out-of-band metadata channels provide a robust architectural pattern for enforcing security governance in AI agent systems by removing security-critical decisions from the agent's control entirely.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly expected to operate as digital employees: accessing enterprise data, making decisions, and taking actions autonomously. But agents are simultaneously less predictable than humans -- prone to hallucination, misinterpretation, and adversarial manipulation -- and more technically capable: with deep system knowledge and high-throughput interfaces cascading damage at machine speed. This combination makes it unsafe to rely on agents to faithfully interpret or propagate security-critical metadata such as access policies, data classifications, and behavioral constraints.
  We present the Redpanda Agentic Data Plane (ADP), an architecture built around out-of-band metadata channels: infrastructure pathways that carry security context, policy signals, and audit trails deterministically, entirely outside the agent's read and write path and across heterogeneous infrastructure. These channels enforce governance at every stage of the agent lifecycle -- scoping data access on the way in, constraining actions during execution, and capturing tamper-proof transcripts on the way out.
  We demonstrate ADP with a multi-agent portfolio rebalancing system in which autonomous agents monitor markets, make trade decisions, and execute orders across isolated client accounts -- with per-client data scoping, trade approval thresholds, and tamper-proof audit trails all enforced by out-of-band channels the agents can neither see nor bypass.

</details>


### [50] [Trends in AI and Human-AI Interaction in Clinical Trials -- A Hybrid Human-AI Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29096)
*Sandra Woolley,Tim Collins,Khalid Khattak,Illia Chernomorets,Ariane Arevalo,Chris Richardson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes temporal and geographical trends in AI-related clinical trials registered on ClinicalTrials.gov, using a hybrid human-GPT-5.5 screening workflow. It finds a marked increase in AI trials, with China and the US leading, and demonstrates that hybrid screening is viable but requires clearer reporting and definitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize temporal trends in AI terminology usage and geographical distribution of AI-related clinical trials, and to explore the feasibility of a hybrid human-AI approach for analyzing human-AI interaction trends in registered trials.

Method: Records were retrieved from ClinicalTrials.gov. A hybrid workflow combining a frontier generative AI model (GPT-5.5) with human review was used to screen and categorize records from an AI-focused search. Inter-rater agreement between human and AI classifiers was assessed on a random sample of 100 records.

Result: Marked increase in AI-related trials over time, with recent growth in ML, deep learning, chatbots, GPTs, and LLMs. China and the US had the largest numbers, with notable recent increases in Italy, France, Spain, UK, and Turkey. Human and AI classifiers showed good agreement on non-substantive AI use but lower agreement on classifying human-AI interaction, especially involving health professionals.

Conclusion: Hybrid human-AI screening of clinical trial records is potentially viable, but clearer trial reporting and more precise interaction definitions are needed to improve the process.

Abstract: This paper examines records retrieved from the ClinicalTrials.gov registry to characterize temporal trends in AI terminology and the geographical distribution of AI trials. The work also reports on an exploratory hybrid human-AI approach to analyzing human-AI interaction trends in registered clinical trials. The hybrid workflow comprised a frontier generative AI model (GPT-5.5) and human review to screen and categorize records returned by an AI-focused search. The findings indicate a marked increase in AI-related trials over time, with recent growth in references to machine learning, deep learning, chatbots, GPTs, and large language models. Geographically, China and the United States accounted for the largest numbers of AI-related trials, with notable recent increases in several other countries including Italy, France, Spain, the UK and Turkey (Türkiye). In a random sample of 100 records, human and AI classifiers showed good agreement in identifying studies not substantively using AI, but lower agreement in classifying human-AI interaction, particularly where health professional interaction was ambiguous or insufficiently described. Overall, the results suggest that hybrid human-AI screening of clinical trial records is potentially viable, but clearer trial reporting and more precise interaction definitions will benefit the process.

</details>


### [51] [Beyond Consensus: Trace-Level Synthesis in Mixture of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29116)
*Shreyas Fadnavis,Praitayini Kanakaraj,Felix Wyss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reveals that aggregating LLM agent outputs at the reasoning trace level, rather than answer-level majority voting, yields superior performance. The authors demonstrate an "aggregation paradox" where an LLM aggregator reading full traces recovers correct solutions even under unanimous agreement, and propose Self-Consistent Mixture of Agents (SC-MoA) which uses perturbation-induced trace diversity and anchored refinement with non-degradation guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard practice of compressing multi-agent reasoning into majority vote or layered synthesis is unnecessarily lossy; treating agreement as the finish line discards valuable information from reasoning traces, especially correct intermediate steps from minority chains.

Method: The authors identify the "aggregation paradox" where an LLM aggregator reading complete reasoning traces outperforms answer-level aggregation. They propose Self-Consistent Mixture of Agents (SC-MoA) which: (1) generates trace diversity via semantic-preserving input perturbations, (2) safeguards majority via anchored refinement with provable non-degradation guarantees, and (3) always synthesizes traces rather than gating on consensus.

Result: A single model with perturbation-induced trace variation outperforms heterogeneous model pools across structured reasoning, PhD-level science, competition mathematics, and competitive programming. Majority voting has a ceiling that perturbation diversity cannot raise (error correlations are identical), while the aggregator's gain comes from trace-level complementarity.

Conclusion: The unit of aggregation should be the reasoning trace, not the answer. Trace-level synthesis unlocks complementarity that answer-level voting cannot achieve.

Abstract: When multiple LLM agents solve the same problem, standard practice compresses each agent's reasoning into a majority vote or layered synthesis, treating agreement as the finish line. We show this is unnecessarily lossy: an LLM aggregator that reads complete reasoning traces recovers correct solutions even when agents unanimously agree, with beneficial corrections consistently outweighing harmful ones -- the \emph{aggregation paradox}.
