TL;DR: Think Fast, Talk Smart is a sleep-health insight pipeline where deterministic code handles recurring analysis before a single LLM writer call, outperforming pure LLM approaches in accuracy, compliance, and cost. It demonstrates that LLMs should be reserved for expressing verified facts within bounded interfaces, while deterministic code should own structured analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used to generate health text from structured records, fluency alone is insufficient—systems must ensure faithfulness to source data, grounded explanations, policy compliance, machine-readable outputs, and low cost. The paper questions which responsibilities in structured health generation should be handled by deterministic computation versus runtime LLM prompting.

Method: The paper introduces Think Fast, Talk Smart, a pipeline that separates concerns: deterministic code performs recurring analysis (comparison, ranking, attribution) before one bounded LLM writer call. Evaluated across 280 user-nights and six models against zero-shot and few-shot one-call baselines. Layer replacement experiments isolate failures at each stage.

Result: The pipeline achieves lower numeric error, lower instruction-compliance error, and lower end-to-end cost than baselines. Layer replacement reveals: LLM comparison increases numeric error, LLM ranking degrades policy selection, LLM attribution increases unsupported causal language, and an LLM-generated writer interface reintroduces errors even with deterministic upstream facts.

Conclusion: The results support a broader design rule: let code own recurring analysis, and let LLMs express verified facts within bounded interfaces.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to generate health text from structured records such as wearable time series, biomarkers, vitals, and care-management logs. For recurring health outputs, fluency is not enough: systems must remain faithful to source data, ground explanatory claims in available evidence, follow stated policies, emit machine-readable outputs, and run cheaply enough for repeated use. We ask which responsibilities in structured health generation should be deterministic computation rather than runtime LLM prompting.
  We introduce Think Fast, Talk Smart, a sleep-health insight pipeline in which deterministic code performs recurring analysis before one bounded LLM writer call. Across 280 user-nights and six models, achieves lower numeric error, lower instruction-compliance error, and lower end-to-end cost than structured zero-shot and few-shot one-call baselines.
  Layer replacement reveals contract-specific failures: LLM comparison raises numeric error, LLM ranking degrades policy selection, LLM attribution increases unsupported causal language, and an LLM-generated writer interface reintroduces errors even after upstream facts are deterministic. The results support a broader design rule: let code own recurring analysis, and let LLMs express verified facts within bounded interfaces.

</details>


### [103] [PTCG-Bench: Can LLM Agents Master Pokémon Trading Card Game?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29653)
*Dongdong Hua,Yifei Sun,Renhong Huang,Feng Gao,Chunping Wang,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PTCG-Bench is a benchmark built on the Pokémon Trading Card Game that evaluates LLM agents on in-game decision-making and self-evolution through accumulated experience, finding that agents achieve non-trivial performance but struggle with sustained self-evolution and are sensitive to harness design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human players can quickly learn and devise strategies in complex board games after few rounds, but autonomous agents lack similar capabilities in realistic interactive environments; existing benchmarks fail to capture strategic and evolving decision-making scenarios.

Method: PTCG-Bench evaluates LLM agents at two levels: (1) decision-making performance within a single complex environment (PTCG), and (2) ability to self-evolve through accumulated experience. A modular harness ablation is included to isolate agent performance from model capability.

Result: LLM agents achieve non-trivial gameplay performance, but sustained and stable self-evolution remains challenging, and performance is sensitive to harness design.

Conclusion: PTCG-Bench can facilitate future research on harness-aware and self-evolving agents in realistic interactive environments.

Abstract: Given a strategically complex board game, human players can quickly learn to devise strategies after playing a few rounds. Autonomous agents require similar capabilities in realistic interactive environments, yet existing agent benchmarks often fail to fully capture such strategic and evolving decision-making scenarios. We present PTCG-Bench, a benchmark built on the Pok'{e}mon Trading Card Game (PTCG) that evaluates LLM agents at two complementary levels: (1) their decision-making performance within a single complex environment, and (2) their ability to self-evolving through accumulated experience. We further include a modular harness ablation to better interpret agent performance without conflating it with model capability. Our experiments show that, although LLM agents can achieve non-trivial gameplay performance, sustained and stable self-evolution remains challenging, and performance is sensitive to harness design. We hope that PTCG-Bench will facilitate future research on harness-aware and self-evolving agents in realistic interactive environments.

</details>


### [104] [TRACE: Toulmin-based Reasoning Assessment through Constructive Elements for LLM CoT Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29656)
*Yundong Kim,Heyoung Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a metric that evaluates LLM reasoning quality by analyzing Chain-of-Thought argument structure using Toulmin's theory and Flavell's metacognitive framework, showing strong correlation with benchmark accuracy (r=0.74) and effectiveness as a reinforcement learning reward signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluation metrics rely on final-answer accuracy or surface-level statistics, leaving the reasoning process itself unexamined, making it challenging to evaluate open-ended outputs where ground truth is absent.

Method: TRACE integrates Toulmin's argumentation theory with Flavell's metacognitive framework to analyze how arguments are constructed in Chain-of-Thought reasoning processes, assessing reasoning structure rather than judging outcomes.

Result: Experiments on 26.3K QA samples across 7 reasoning models show strong correlation with benchmark accuracy (r=0.74); TRACE also outperforms accuracy-only baselines when used as a reinforcement learning reward signal.

Conclusion: Logically sound reasoning leads to higher-quality answers, and TRACE serves as a complementary metric for evaluating open-ended LLM outputs.

Abstract: Evaluating open-ended outputs from large language models (LLMs) remains challenging due to the absence of ground truth. Existing metrics rely on final-answer accuracy or surface-level statistics, leaving the reasoning process itself unexamined. We introduce TRACE (Toulmin-based Reasoning Assessment through Constructive Elements), a metric that analyzes Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning processes. Rather than judging outcomes, TRACE inspects how arguments are constructed by integrating Toulmin's argumentation theory with Flavell's metacognitive framework to assess reasoning structure. Experiments on 26.3K QA samples across 7 reasoning models show strong correlation with benchmark accuracy (r=0.74). Furthermore, TRACE is effective as a reinforcement learning reward signal, outperforming accuracy-only baselines. Together, these results indicate that logically sound reasoning leads to higher-quality answers. TRACE thus serves as a complementary metric for evaluating open-ended outputs. Code is available at https://github.com/hyyangkisti/trace.

</details>


### [105] [GRASP: Gated Regression-Aware Skill Proposer for Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29668)
*Johannes Moll,Jean-Philippe Corbeil,Jiazhen Pan,Martin Hadamitzky,Daniel Rueckert,Lisa Adams,Keno Bressem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRASP introduces a gated regression-aware method for self-improving LLM agents in structured environments, ensuring each new skill doesn't regress prior performance. It achieves substantial gains across multiple models on clinical benchmarks and generalizes to non-clinical domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior self-improvement methods for LLM agents in structured environments accumulate natural-language guidance without verifying that new skills don't break previously correct behaviors, causing silent regressions.

Method: GRASP treats agent improvement as a sequence of edits to a bounded skill library, admitting a candidate only if it yields net improvement on a balanced held-out probe under a hard regression budget. It uses comparative proposal generation, an acceptance gate, and a hard regression budget.

Result: On MedAgentBench, GRASP lifts gpt-oss-120b from 40.6% to 88.8%, outperforms the strongest baseline by 21.0 points, and improves all models by 17.2–40.3 points. Ablations show the gate and regression budget drive gains, not skill writing alone. Generalizes to 3 of 4 non-clinical environments. Frozen skill libraries transfer asymmetrically: stronger→weaker works, reverse does not.

Conclusion: Gated regression-aware skill acquisition is effective for reliable LLM agent self-improvement in structured environments, with asymmetric cross-model transferability, but limited in open-ended action spaces.

Abstract: LLM agents acting in structured environments fail in operational rather than conversational ways, and reliability depends on procedural knowledge of the environment. Prior self-improvement methods accumulate natural-language guidance without checking that each new item preserves previously correct behavior, so a note that fixes one trajectory can silently regress another. We introduce GRASP (Gated Regression-Aware Skill Proposer), which treats agent improvement as a sequence of edits to a bounded skill library, admitting each candidate only if it produces a net improvement on a balanced held-out probe under a hard regression budget. We evaluate GRASP across five base models (gpt-oss-120b, DeepSeek V4 Flash, Gemini 3.1 Flash Lite, GPT-4.1, GPT-5.4) on two FHIR-based clinical benchmarks. On MedAgentBench, GRASP lifts gpt-oss-120b from 40.6% to 88.8%, exceeds the strongest of five self-improvement baselines by 21.0 points, and improves every other base model by 17.2 to 40.3 points. Ablations attribute the gain to comparative proposal generation, the acceptance gate, and the hard regression budget rather than to skill writing itself, which without validation is no better than using no skills. The mechanism generalizes beyond the clinical domain, improving agents on three of four non-clinical environments and remaining flat only where the action space is open-ended. Frozen libraries transfer across models, where skills from a stronger model improve weaker executors beyond what they learn for themselves while the reverse does not, an asymmetry that no ungated baseline reproduces.

</details>


### [106] [NICE: A Theory-Grounded Diagnostic Benchmark for Social Intelligence of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29685)
*Yunjin Qi,Zhaojun Jiang,Xuan Wu,Hanxi Pan,Yixuan Wang,Yanfang Liu,Xiang Ji,Churu Yu,Chunyuan Zheng,Yingze Chen,Jie He,Liuqing Chen,Zaifeng Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces NICE, a theory-grounded diagnostic benchmark for evaluating social intelligence in LLMs, organized into 4 categories and 11 dimensions across 137 items. It reveals that frontier LLMs excel in aggregate accuracy but consistently underperform in Communication, specifically in multi-turn communication, nonverbal communication, and synchrony.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing social intelligence benchmarks lack a unified theoretical framework, preventing fine-grained diagnosis of LLMs' social abilities. As LLMs are increasingly used in social contexts like emotional companionship and customer service, a holistic, theory-driven evaluation is critical for ensuring quality and safety in human-AI interaction.

Method: The authors constructed a social intelligence framework via literature review and multi-stage expert validation following psychometric principles, resulting in 4 categories and 11 dimensions with fine-grained capability facets. They then operationalized this framework into NICE, a diagnostic benchmark of 137 items in Chinese contexts, and evaluated 5 frontier LLMs alongside a human reference group.

Result: Frontier LLMs achieved higher aggregate accuracy scores but exhibited a consistent weakness in Communication. The framework localized this deficit to three specific capability facets: multi-turn communication, nonverbal communication, and synchrony.

Conclusion: NICE reframes social intelligence evaluation from aggregate scoring toward theory-grounded diagnosis, enabling precise identification of socially consequential weaknesses in LLMs that aggregate metrics would otherwise obscure.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in social contexts such as emotional companionship and customer service, measuring their social intelligence has become critical to the quality and safety of human-AI interaction. However, existing social intelligence benchmarks lack a unified framework that organizes social abilities into a unified structure, and therefore cannot enable fine-grained diagnosis. To build the first holistic diagnostic evaluation grounded in social theory, we first construct a social intelligence framework through a literature review and multi-stage expert validation guided by psychometric principles. The resulting framework includes 4 categories and 11 dimensions, each further specified by fine-grained capability facets. Building on this framework, we introduce NICE (Norm, Interaction, Cognition, Experience), a diagnostic benchmark of 137 items operationalized through representative Chinese contexts. Across 5 frontier LLMs and a human reference group, models score higher in aggregate accuracy yet show a consistent weakness in Communication, which the framework localizes to 3 specific capability facets: multi-turn communication, nonverbal communication, and synchrony. NICE thus reframes social intelligence evaluation toward theory-grounded diagnosis of socially consequential weaknesses in LLMs.

</details>


### [107] [Reliable Reasoning with Large Language Models via Preference-Based Maximum Satisfiability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29687)
*Pedro Orvalho,Marta Kwiatkowska,Guillem Alenyà,Felip Manyà*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs generate Python code encoding constraints as preference-based MaxSAT problems, solved by exact solvers with independent verification, achieving >80% acceptance rates on optimisation tasks where baselines rarely produce feasible solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with multi-constraint optimisation tasks involving user-defined preferences, common in robotics and other domains.

Method: A hybrid approach where LLMs externalise reasoning via code generation: given a natural language problem, an LLM produces Python code encoding constraints/preferences as a preference-based MaxSAT problem, solved by an exact MaxSAT solver. Solutions are independently verified for feasibility and optimality against a canonical MaxSAT encoding.

Result: The MaxSAT-based pipeline achieves substantially higher acceptance rates than direct-answer, chain-of-thought, and program-of-thought baselines, exceeding 80% in some cases.

Conclusion: LLM-driven code generation combined with preference-based MaxSAT enables solver-verifiable optimisation and substantially improves correctness under independently verified reference semantics.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at understanding natural language but struggle with optimisation tasks involving multiple constraints and user-defined preferences, which commonly arise in domains such as robotics. We propose a hybrid reasoning approach in which LLMs externalise reasoning through code generation. Given a natural language problem description, an LLM generates Python code that encodes user-defined constraints and preferences as a preference-based Maximum Satisfiability (MaxSAT) problem, which is then solved by an exact MaxSAT solver. To ensure correctness, solutions returned by the model-generated code are independently verified for feasibility and optimality against a canonical MaxSAT encoding, allowing for different encodings and multiple optimal solutions. We evaluate our approach using both open-source and closed-access LLMs on three families of preference-based reasoning tasks, and compare it against direct-answer, chain-of-thought, and program-of-thought baselines using the same models. While these baselines rarely produce feasible solutions, the MaxSAT-based pipeline achieves substantially higher acceptance rates, in some cases exceeding 80%. Our results demonstrate that LLM-driven code generation combined with preference-based MaxSAT enables solver-verifiable optimisation with respect to generated encodings, and substantially improves correctness under independently verified reference semantics.

</details>


### [108] [FHRFormer: A Self-Supervised Masked Transformer Framework for Fetal Heart Rate Time-Series Inpainting and Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29695)
*Kjersti Engan,Neel Kanwal,Anita Yeconia,Ladislaus Blacy,Yuda Munyaw,Estomih Mduma,Hege Ersdal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a masked transformer-based autoencoder to reconstruct missing fetal heart rate signals caused by sensor displacement in wearable monitors, enabling robust AI-based risk analysis for newborns needing breathing assistance at birth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fetal heart rate monitoring is critical for assessing fetal well-being and predicting the need for breathing assistance at birth. However, wearable FHR monitors suffer from signal dropout due to sensor displacement during maternal/fetal movement, creating missing data gaps that limit meaningful AI-based analysis. Traditional interpolation fails to preserve spectral signal characteristics.

Method: A masked transformer-based autoencoder approach that captures both local temporal and frequency components of FHR signals to reconstruct missing data. The method supports signal inpainting and forecasting.

Result: The proposed method demonstrates robustness across varying durations of missing data, outperforming traditional interpolation techniques in preserving spectral characteristics.

Conclusion: The approach can be applied retrospectively to research datasets for developing AI-based risk algorithms, and could be integrated into wearable FHR monitors for earlier, more robust risk detection.

Abstract: Approximately 10% of newborns require assistance to initiate breathing at birth, and around 5% need ventilation support. Fetal heart rate (FHR) monitoring plays a crucial role in assessing fetal well-being during prenatal care, enabling the detection of abnormal patterns and supporting timely obstetric interventions to mitigate fetal risks during labor. Applying artificial intelligence (AI) methods to analyze large datasets of continuous FHR monitoring episodes with diverse outcomes may offer novel insights into predicting the risk of needing breathing assistance or interventions. Recent advances in wearable FHR monitors have enabled continuous fetal monitoring without compromising maternal mobility. However, sensor displacement during maternal movement, as well as changes in fetal or maternal position, often lead to signal dropout, resulting in gaps in recorded FHR data. Such missing data limits the extraction of meaningful insights and complicates automated (AI-based) analysis. Traditional approaches to handling missing data, such as simple interpolation techniques, often fail to preserve the spectral characteristics of the signals. In this paper, we propose a masked transformer-based autoencoder approach to reconstruct missing FHR signals by capturing both local temporal and frequency components of the data. The proposed method demonstrates robustness across varying durations of missing data and can be used for signal inpainting and forecasting. The proposed approach can be applied retrospectively to research datasets to support the development of AI-based risk algorithms. In the future, the proposed method could be integrated into wearable FHR monitoring devices to achieve earlier and more robust risk detection.

</details>


### [109] [Beyond Trajectory Rewards: Step-level Credit Assignment for Agentic Search via Graph Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29697)
*Yuchen Liu,Yingjie Feng,Lixiong Qin,Jiasi Chen,Jianing Yu,Sheng Gao,Sheng Yang,Weiran Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GDCR uses entity-relation graph distances to provide step-level rewards in Agentic Search, combined with SAPO for policy optimization, outperforming on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory-level outcome rewards fail to quantify individual step contributions in Agentic Search, and existing step-level reward methods rely on costly tree sampling.

Method: Proposes Graph-Distance Contribution Reward (GDCR) that scores newly-retrieved/cited entities by their distance to the answer node in a training-time Entity-Relation (ER) graph, and Step Advantage Policy Optimization (SAPO) that converts GDCR into step-level advantages combined with trajectory-level outcome advantages.

Result: Validated effectiveness on four challenging benchmarks.

Conclusion: Graph-distance-based step rewards offer an efficient and effective alternative to costly tree sampling for step-level credit assignment in Agentic Search.

Abstract: In Agentic Search, trajectory-level outcome rewards fail to quantify the behavioral contributions of individual steps, while existing step-level reward methods typically rely on costly tree sampling. We view world knowledge as a latent world graph and each IS task as search within a latent task graph, where effective steps should make graph progress toward the answer node. Based on this prior, we propose Graph-Distance Contribution Reward (GDCR), a step-level process reward that scores newly-retrieved and newly-cited entities by their distance to the answer node in a training-time Entity-Relation (ER) graph. We further propose Step Advantage Policy Optimization (SAPO), which converts GDCR into step-level advantages and combines them with trajectory-level outcome advantages. Experiments on four challenging benchmarks validate the effectiveness of our method.

</details>


### [110] [BitTP: The Lightweight Trajectory Prediction Model with BitLLM for Edge-Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29705)
*Mincheol Kang,Hyunjin Lim,Bomin Kang,Daehee Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BitTP converts LLM-based trajectory predictors into lightweight 1.58-bit weight-quantized architectures that preserve full-precision activations, reducing memory and latency while improving prediction accuracy (ADE by 14.29%, FDE by 20.97%) over full-precision baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based trajectory predictors offer strong reasoning but are too memory- and compute-intensive for deployment on resource-constrained edge devices like autonomous robot onboard computers.

Method: Proposes BitTP, which applies weight-only quantization to 1.58-bit (bitlinear architecture) while keeping activations in full precision, as quantizing activations degrades spatio-temporal reasoning.

Result: BitTP-Weight reduces ADE by 14.29% and FDE by 20.97% on average compared to the full-precision BF16 LLM baseline, while also cutting memory usage and inference latency relative to other quantization methods.

Conclusion: Carefully designed quantization (weight-only 1.58-bit) acts as an effective regularizer, enabling practical deployment of sophisticated LLM-based trajectory reasoning on edge devices.

Abstract: Trajectory prediction is a fundamental task for autonomous systems, requiring complex reasoning about multi-agent interactions and intents. Large language models (LLMs) have recently been adopted for this task, as they provide strong contextual reasoning and interpretable, language-based trajectory representations. However, these LLM-based predictors are extremely memory- and compute-intensive, making them difficult to deploy on resource-constrained edge devices such as on-board computers in autonomous robots. To bridge this gap, we propose BitTP, which converts an LLM-based trajectory predictor into a lightweight bitlinear architecture. We demonstrate that weight-only quantization to 1.58-bit (BitTP-Weight) is optimal. Crucially, activations must remain in full precision, as quantizing them leads to severe degradation and instability in spatio-temporal reasoning. Empirically, BitTP-Weight not only preserves but improves prediction quality over the full-precision (BF16) LLM baseline, reducing ADE by 14.29% and FDE by 20.97% on average, while simultaneously reducing memory usage and inference latency relative to other quantization methods. These results demonstrate that carefully designed quantization acts as an effective regularizer, enabling the practical deployment of sophisticated LLM-based reasoning on edge devices. Code is available at: https://github.com/MintCat98/BitTP.

</details>


### [111] [NaRA: Noise-Aware LoRA for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of Diffusion LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29716)
*Shuaidi Wang,Zhan Zhuang,Ruping Huang,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Diffusion LLMs need noise-aware PEFT; NaRA uses a hypernetwork conditioned on noise level to generate dynamic low-rank updates, outperforming static methods like LoRA on reasoning and code tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PEFT methods (e.g., LoRA) are designed for autoregressive models and use static parameters that are oblivious to the noise level in diffusion models, ignoring the fact that input distributions and generation difficulty vary significantly along the denoising trajectory. This makes them suboptimal for diffusion LLMs.

Method: NaRA (Noise-aware Low-Rank Adaptation) introduces a low-rank core matrix generated by a lightweight, globally shared hypernetwork that conditions on the noise level, enabling update matrices to vary continuously along the diffusion process with minimal parameter and latency overhead.

Result: NaRA achieves consistent improvements over noise-agnostic baselines across commonsense reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks.

Conclusion: Noise-aware dynamic adaptation is crucial for effective PEFT in diffusion LLMs, and NaRA provides a theoretically justified, efficient solution that outperforms static approaches.

Abstract: Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising non-autoregressive generative paradigm. Given the prohibitive computational cost of full fine-tuning, Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has become the standard approach. However, existing PEFT methods (e.g., LoRA), originally tailored for autoregressive models, rely on static parameters that are agnostic to the noise level. Consequently, they ignore the intrinsic dynamics of the diffusion process, where input distributions and generation difficulty shift significantly along the denoising trajectory, rendering them suboptimal for dLLMs. To address this, we propose Noise-aware Low-Rank Adaptation (NaRA), which introduces a low-rank core matrix generated by a lightweight, globally shared hypernetwork conditioned on the noise level. This design enables the update matrices to vary continuously along the diffusion process while keeping parameter and latency overhead negligible. We provide a theoretical justification for the proposed NaRA framework and empirically demonstrate consistent improvements over noise-agnostic baselines across commonsense reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks. Our code is available at https://github.com/generaldi/NaRA.

</details>


### [112] [Uncertainty-Aware Transfer Learning for Cross-Building Energy Forecasting: Toward Robust and Scalable District-Level Energy Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29733)
*Shadmehr Zaregarizi,Khashayar Yavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An uncertainty-aware transfer learning framework using Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) for cross-building energy forecasting, evaluated on source (Aalborg University) and target (NEST building) datasets. Introduces Transfer Robustness Index (TRI) metric. Probe-Only fine-tuning (455/806K parameters) achieves best transfer quality (TRI=3,097). MC Dropout yields 93.2% prediction interval coverage. Data-scarcity analysis shows monotonic improvement with more target data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for models that can be re-used across buildings with minimal target-domain data and honest uncertainty estimates to scale data-driven energy forecasting to district level.

Method: 1) Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) as backbone; 2) Transfer Robustness Index (TRI) metric for quantifying generalization quality across domain gaps; 3) Four-strategy layer-freezing ablation (including Probe-Only fine-tuning); 4) Monte Carlo Dropout for uncertainty quantification.

Result: Probe-Only fine-tuning (455 output-layer parameters updated) outperforms full fine-tuning with TRI=3,097. MC Dropout achieves 93.2% prediction interval coverage probability (close to 95% nominal). Monotonic improvement with increasing target-domain data.

Conclusion: TFT encoders learn transferable temporal representations; minimal parameter updates suffice for cross-building transfer; the framework provides practical guidance for district-level energy deployment.

Abstract: Scaling data-driven energy forecasting to district level requires models that can be re-used across buildings with minimal target-domain data and honest uncertainty estimates. We present an uncertainty-aware transfer learning (TL) framework for cross-building energy forecasting based on the Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT), evaluated on a newly released high-resolution real sub-meter dataset: an educational building at Aalborg University, Denmark (source) and the multi-typology NEST building at EMPA, Switzerland (target). We introduce the Transfer Robustness Index (TRI), an architecture-agnostic metric for quantifying generalization quality across domain gaps. A four-strategy layer-freezing ablation shows that Probe-Only fine-tuning, updating only 455 output-layer parameters out of 806K, achieves the best transfer quality (TRI = 3,097), outperforming full fine-tuning and suggesting that TFT encoders learn transferable temporal representations. Monte Carlo Dropout yields a prediction interval coverage probability of 93.2%, close to the nominal 95% target. A data-scarcity analysis further shows monotonic improvement with increasing target-domain data, providing practical guidance for district energy deployment.

</details>


### [113] [Citation-Closure Retrieval and Per-Rule Attribution for Real-World Regulatory Compliance Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29742)
*Yeong-Joon Ju,Seong-Whan Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RegOps-Bench, a benchmark for Regulatory Compliance QA, and RefWalk, a framework that uses a shared topic anchor to traverse cross-document citations and enforce per-rule attribution, achieving significant improvements in retrieval recall and citation accuracy over existing RAG systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems fail at regulatory compliance tasks due to flattened citation edges, fragmented retrieval expansions, and fragile post-hoc attribution, while traditional multi-hop or legal QA methods are ill-suited for structured procedural lookups and evidence-set closure required in multi-tiered regulatory authority structures.

Method: RefWalk is a unified framework driven by a shared topic anchor that traverses cross-document citations, fuses multi-view candidates via max-based aggregation, and enforces per-rule attribution to explicitly map claims to sources. The paper also formalizes RegOps-Bench, a benchmark with an Operational Knowledge Graph derived from complex national R&D regulations.

Result: RefWalk establishes a strong baseline with substantial improvements in retrieval recall and citation accuracy. A contrastive evaluation on HIPAA reveals that existing systems exhibit saturation on flat-structure rules, underscoring the need for RegOps-Bench.

Conclusion: Regulatory compliance QA requires specialized benchmarks and methods beyond traditional RAG; RefWalk effectively addresses citation traversal and attribution challenges, and RegOps-Bench exposes limitations of existing systems on structured regulatory tasks.

Abstract: Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) for regulatory compliance demands rigorous traceability via comprehensive citations across multi-tiered authority structures. Unlike traditional multi-hop or legal QA, this task requires structured procedural lookups and evidence-set closure rather than entity resolution or case-law reasoning. Existing RAG systems struggle here due to flattened citation edges, fragmented retrieval expansions, and fragile post-hoc attribution. We formalize Regulatory Compliance QA with RegOps-Bench, a novel benchmark featuring an Operational Knowledge Graph derived from complex national R\&D regulations. To address these bottlenecks, we propose RefWalk, a unified framework driven by a shared topic anchor. RefWalk traverses cross-document citations, fuses multi-view candidates via max-based aggregation, and enforces per-rule attribution to explicitly map claims to sources. We establish a strong baseline with substantial improvements in retrieval recall and citation accuracy. Finally, a contrastive evaluation on a U.S. health compliance dataset (HIPAA) reveals that existing systems exhibit saturation on flat-structure rules, underscoring the need for RegOps-Bench. Our code is available at https://github.com/yeongjoonJu/RefWalk.

</details>


### [114] [Why Specialist Models Still Matter: A Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Paradigm for Medical Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29744)
*Yanan Wang,Shuaicong Hu,Jian Liu,Guohui Zhou,Aiguo Wang,Cuiwei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HetMedAgent is a heterogeneous multi-agent framework that orchestrates collaboration between generalist LLMs, domain-specific specialist models, and clinicians for clinical decision-making, demonstrating that synergy between model types outperforms using either alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether domain-specific medical specialist models will become obsolete given the impressive performance of generalist LLMs in healthcare, and to propose that the future lies in multi-agent collaboration rather than monolithic foundation models or replacing human expertise.

Method: HetMedAgent framework with three key components: conflict-aware evidence fusion, uncertainty-based clinician intervention triggering, and adaptive threshold calibration, operating as a heterogeneous medical multi-agent system.

Result: On three real-world clinical decision-making tasks, the synergy between generalist LLMs and domain-specific specialist models significantly outperforms using either type of model alone, validating the irreplaceable value of specialist models in modality-specific analysis.

Conclusion: The future of medical AI lies in multi-agent collaboration rather than building monolithic medical foundation models, achieving a balance between general reasoning capabilities and domain-specific precision.

Abstract: The impressive performance of generalist large language models (LLMs) such as GPT and Claude in healthcare raises a critical question: will domain-specific medical specialist models become obsolete? We argue that the future of medical artificial intelligence (AI) lies not in building monolithic medical foundation models, nor in replacing human expertise, but in orchestrating collaboration among generalist LLMs, domain-specific specialist models, and clinicians. We propose HetMedAgent, a heterogeneous medical multi-agent framework that enables conflict-aware evidence fusion, uncertainty-based clinician intervention triggering, and adaptive threshold calibration. Experiments on three real-world clinical decision-making tasks demonstrate that the synergy between generalist LLMs and domain-specific specialist models significantly outperforms using either type of model alone, validating the irreplaceable value of specialist models in modality-specific analysis. HetMedAgent represents a shift from building medical LLMs or foundation models to multi-agent collaboration, achieving a balance between general reasoning capabilities and domain-specific precision.

</details>


### [115] [Benchmarking Positional Encoding Strategies for Transformer-Based EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29754)
*Ayse Betul Yuce,Sebastian Stober*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks five positional encoding strategies for transformer-based EEG foundation models, finding that optimal encoding is task-dependent—SPE works best for motor imagery while ACPE is more consistent across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised EEG decoding models struggle to generalize across tasks, subjects, and datasets, motivating the development of transformer-based EEG foundation models trained with self-supervised learning. Since transformers are permutation-invariant, they require explicit positional information, and unlike textual tokens, EEG electrodes are spatially distributed across the scalp, raising the question of how electrode positions should be encoded.

Method: Benchmarked five positional encoding strategies within the CBraMod backbone under linear probing and fine-tuning protocols on motor imagery classification and emotion recognition tasks.

Result: No single encoding strategy consistently outperforms across tasks. SPE yields strong representations for motor imagery but underperforms on emotion recognition, while ACPE demonstrates more consistent performance across tasks.

Conclusion: The optimal positional encoding strategy is task-dependent, with no universal solution across EEG decoding scenarios.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is a widely used non-invasive technique for measuring brain activity in brain-computer interface (BCI) applications. Supervised EEG decoding models often struggle to generalize across tasks, subjects, and datasets, motivating transformer-based EEG foundation models trained with self-supervised learning. Since transformers are permutation-invariant, they require explicit positional information. Unlike textual tokens, EEG electrodes are spatially distributed across the scalp, raising the question of how electrode positions should be encoded in transformer-based EEG models. In this study, we benchmark five positional encoding strategies within the CBraMod backbone and evaluate them under linear probing and fine-tuning protocols on motor imagery classification and emotion recognition. Our results show that no single strategy consistently outperforms across tasks. Spherical Positional Encoding (SPE) yields strong representations for motor imagery but underperforms on emotion recognition, while Asymmetric Conditional Positional Encoding (ACPE) demonstrates more consistent performance across tasks. These findings suggest that the optimal positional encoding strategy is task-dependent, with no universal solution across EEG decoding scenarios.

</details>


### [116] [LFQ: Logit-aware Final-block Quantization for Boosting the Generation Quality of Low-Bit Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29756)
*Jung Hyun Lee,June Yong Yang,Jungwook Choi,Eunho Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LFQ improves block-wise post-training quantization for LLMs by quantizing the final Transformer block with cross-entropy loss on logits, fixing token probability misalignment caused by omitting the LM head and using MSE, thus enhancing generative task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bit weight-only PTQ degrades generative task quality (especially long responses and chain-of-thought) due to (i) excluding the unembedding layer from block-wise optimization and (ii) using MSE loss, both causing token probability distribution misalignment between quantized and full-precision models.

Method: Logit-aware Final-block Quantization (LFQ): quantizes the final Transformer block by minimizing cross-entropy between the logits of the FP model and the quantized model, aligning token probabilities at the logit level.

Result: LFQ consistently improves accuracy on complex generation tasks over state-of-the-art block-wise PTQ across diverse model families, while maintaining parity with FP baselines on language modeling and understanding.

Conclusion: Simple enhancement to block-wise PTQ that rectifies token probability misalignment by logit-level alignment in the final block, significantly improving generative task performance without sacrificing quality on basic tasks.

Abstract: As large language models continue to scale, low-bit weight-only post-training quantization (PTQ) offers a practical solution to their memory-efficient deployment. Although block-wise PTQ is capable of matching the full-precision (FP) baseline on basic language modeling and understanding, its quality is degraded for generative tasks -- especially at longer responses and extended chains of thought, which is critical in boosting task accuracy. We attribute this shortfall to two factors: (i) the omission of the unembedding layer (the LM head) in block-wise optimization and (ii) the reliance on the mean squared error (MSE) objective. Both factors cause the token probability distribution of the quantized model to misalign with that of the FP model, yielding notable accuracy drops on text generation benchmarks. To rectify the discrepancy, we introduce Logit-aware Final-block Quantization (LFQ), a simple yet effective enhancement to block-wise PTQ that quantizes the final Transformer block by minimizing the cross-entropy between the logits of the FP model and those of its quantized counterpart. By aligning token probabilities at the logit level in the final block, LFQ consistently improves the accuracy of complex generation tasks over state-of-the-art block-wise PTQ across diverse model families, while maintaining parity with FP baselines on language modeling and understanding.

</details>


### [117] [From XXLTraffic to EvoXXLTraffic: Scaling Traffic Forecasting to Sensor-Evolving Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29768)
*Du Yin,Hao Xue,Arian Prabowo,Shuang Ao,Flora Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces XXLTraffic (up to 27 years of traffic data) and EvoXXLTraffic, a sensor-evolving reorganization of that data covering 9 PeMS districts with growth ratios up to 10,000%+. They define a yearly streaming forecasting protocol and benchmark various baselines, finding that many SOTA static methods fail on this more realistic evolutionary setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing traffic forecasting benchmarks assume a fixed sensor set, but real road-sensor networks grow continuously as the road network changes year by year, creating a gap between benchmark assumptions and real-world deployment.

Method: The authors construct the XXLTraffic dataset family (27 years, California PeMS + Transport for NSW), then extend to EvoXXLTraffic by reorganizing data into per-year active sensors, yearly traffic-flow matrices, and yearly graph snapshots across nine PeMS districts. They define a yearly streaming forecasting protocol (each calendar year as a continual task) and benchmark static spatio-temporal GNNs, online schemes, evolving-graph continual methods, and retrieval/test-time methods.

Result: The ultra-large evolutionary dataset reveals that many SOTA static methods no longer work well under sensor-evolving conditions, showing the dataset better reflects real-world traffic forecasting challenges.

Conclusion: The EvoXXLTraffic dataset complements existing benchmarks by enabling more realistic forecasting under ultra-long evolutionary road networks, filling a critical gap in traffic forecasting evaluation.

Abstract: Existing traffic forecasting benchmarks assume a fixed sensor set, but real road-sensor networks grow continuously as the road network changes year by year. We introduce the XXLTraffic dataset family, which spans up to 27 years of California PeMS and Transport for NSW data. The fixed-sensor subsets of XXLTraffic support extremely long forecasting with multi-year gaps and standard hourly / daily long-horizon forecasting. We extend it to EvoXXLTraffic, a sensor-evolving reorganization that exposes per-year active sensors, yearly traffic-flow matrices, and yearly graph snapshots across nine PeMS districts, with growth ratios ranging from +305% to over +10,000%. We define a yearly streaming forecasting protocol on EvoXXLTraffic in which each calendar year is a continual task, and benchmark a wide range of representative baselines drawn from static spatio-temporal GNNs, naïve online schemes, evolving-graph continual methods, and retrieval / test-time methods. We find that our ultra-large evolutionary dataset better reflects the real world, and many state-of-the-art (SOTA) results no longer work. Our dataset complements existing benchmarks by enabling more realistic forecasting under ultra-long evolutionary road networks.

</details>


### [118] [Croissant Tasks: A Metadata Format for Reproducible Machine Learning Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29786)
*Omar Benjelloun,Leonardo Martins Bianco,Isabelle Guyon,Thanh Gia Hieu Khuong,Jonathan Lebensold,Sebastian Lobentanzer,Luis Oala,Benedictus Kent Rachmat,Ihsan Ullah,Peyman Vahidi,Joaquin Vanschoren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Croissant Tasks is a declarative, machine-actionable metadata format that abstracts ML implementation details into high-level task specifications, enabling conceptual reproducibility via automated agent-generated implementations rather than brittle source code replication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducibility is critical yet remains challenging in ML due to underspecified execution details and brittle software environments. Human-centric remedies like checklists are effort-intensive and fail to scale.

Method: (1) The Croissant Tasks specification that formally decouples task problem from solution; (2) an automated LLM pipeline to retrofit existing benchmarks into this format; (3) empirical validation of autonomous agents ingesting these specifications to generate functional reproduction pipelines.

Result: Autonomous agents can ingest Croissant Tasks specifications to generate functional, accurate reproduction pipelines from scratch.

Conclusion: The Croissant Tasks format is envisioned as a new foundation for automated and conceptual reproducibility in machine learning.

Abstract: Reproducibility is fundamental to the scientific method, yet remains a critical challenge in machine learning. Contributing factors include underspecified execution details and brittle software environments. Human-centric remedies, such as checklists and manual verification, help but require intensive effort and fail to scale. To address this, we introduce Croissant Tasks: a declarative, machine-actionable metadata format that abstracts low-level implementation details into high-level specifications. This format enables conceptual reproducibility: verifying claims via independent, agent-generated implementations rather than brittle source code replication. We contribute: (1) the Croissant Tasks specification, formally decoupling task problem from solution; (2) an automated LLM pipeline that retrofits existing benchmarks into this format; and (3) empirical validation showing autonomous agents can ingest these specifications to generate functional, accurate reproduction pipelines from scratch. We envision this format as a new foundation for automated and conceptual reproducibility in machine learning.

</details>


### [119] [Certified Policy Optimisation for Nested Causal Bandits via PAC-Bayes Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29788)
*Tim Woydt,Paul-David Zuercher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs) to model causally coupled hierarchical decision-making across timescales, proposes Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS) with mechanism-factorized posteriors, derives a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound for off-policy certification, and demonstrates significant transfer, dominance, and certificate contraction in experiments, leading to progressive certified handover for safe deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory assumes single-timescale decisions and fails to capture the causal coupling where strategic decisions shape the context for all subsequent tactical choices. The paper formalizes this gap as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs).

Method: Formalizes NCCBs as a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution. Proposes Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS) which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively. Derives a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound for off-policy, anytime certification of deployment policies from historic data.

Result: Against RFF-GP joint regression, NCTS shows significantly better zero-shot transfer under exogenous distribution shifts, recursive meta-to-inner commit dominates joint-commit alternatives in distribution, and the certificate contracts as offline data accumulates. Establishes progressive certified handover for safe deployment.

Conclusion: The factorized SCM-mechanism posterior enables safer hierarchical decision-making with certified risk bounds, enabling timescale-independent transition from legacy controllers to learned policies when gains can be certified.

Abstract: Critical sequential decisions are rarely single-timescale: a strategic decision causally shapes the context in which every subsequent tactical choice is made; standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture this causal coupling between timescales. We formalise the problem class as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs), a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution, and propose Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS), which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively under it. Our main theoretical result is a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound that certifies any candidate deployment policy from historic data alone, off-policy and anytime, answering the deployment question: can we trust this agent here, and at what risk? Experiments on a hierarchical SCM show that, against a matched RFF-GP joint regression on the same function class, the factorised SCM-mechanism posterior transfers significantly better zero-shot under exogenous distribution shifts, the recursive meta-to-inner commit significantly dominates the joint-commit alternative in distribution, and the certificate significantly contracts as offline data accumulates. Combining these results, we establish progressive certified handover, a safe-deployment method: each timescale flips from a legacy controller to NCTS when gains can be certified, independently of the others.

</details>


### [120] [SkillsInjector: Dynamic Skill Context Construction for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29794)
*Yanchao Li,Wanhao Liu,Ben Gao,Jiaqing Xie,Zhehong Ai,Na Zou,Yuqiang Li,Tianfan Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Existing LLM agents treat skill injection as a static process (fixed selection, budget, descriptions), which can harm performance. SkillsInjector adaptively selects skills, determines their number, and tailors descriptions based on context, achieving substantial gains across three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods inject skills into LLM agents statically—with fixed selection criteria, predetermined budgets, and unchanged descriptions—which can degrade task performance rather than improve it. The authors argue that decisions about which skills to expose, how many, and how they are presented all affect downstream outcomes.

Method: SkillsInjector is a two-stage adaptive method: (1) a context planner learns execution-grounded skill preferences and dynamically determines the number of skills per task; (2) a set-aware renderer tailors how selected skill descriptions are presented relative to co-injected neighboring skills.

Result: Across tau2-bench, SkillsBench, and ALFWorld, SkillsInjector achieves the highest scores, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.9, 6.1, and 7.3 percentage points respectively. Ablations confirm that skill selection, adaptive budgeting, and set-aware rendering each contribute to the improvement.

Conclusion: Skill-augmented LLM agents benefit from optimizing the injected context itself rather than treating skill injection as a static step.

Abstract: LLM agents now draw on growing skill libraries to handle complex tasks. However, injecting more skills does not always improve task completion and can even degrade it. Existing methods still treat skill injection as a static step, selecting skills with fixed criteria, fixing the budget in advance, and leaving descriptions unchanged. We argue that this static treatment can undermine the utility of skills, because which skills are exposed, how many are included, and how they are presented all affect downstream performance. We propose SkillsInjector, a two-stage adaptive method that jointly addresses these decisions. First, a context planner learns execution-grounded skill preferences and admits an adaptive number of skills for each task. A set-aware renderer then tailors how selected descriptions are presented relative to their co-injected neighbors. Across tau2-bench, SkillsBench, and ALFWorld, SkillsInjector achieves the highest score, improving over the strongest baseline by 3.9, 6.1, and 7.3 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies show that skill selection, adaptive budgeting, and set-aware rendering each contribute to the gain. These results show that skill-augmented agents benefit from optimizing the injected context itself. Code will be released upon publication

</details>


### [121] [MEMENTO: Leveraging Web as a Learning Signal for Low-Data Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29795)
*Ashutosh Ojha,Vinay Aggarwal,Ashutosh Srivastava,Siddharth Yedlapati,Yaman K Singla,Jitendra Ajmera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEMENTO is a framework that treats the web as a learning signal (rather than a retrieval interface) for low-data tasks, using iterative exploration trees and dual-channel memory to improve performance in sales automation (+25.6%) and legal research (+36.5%) without additional model training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing low-data learning approaches (few-shot prompting, instruction tuning, synthetic data) still rely on labeled/pseudo-labeled data as the primary signal, failing to emulate how humans learn through self-directed web interaction and iterative refinement of knowledge and strategies.

Method: MEMENTO operates at two levels: (1) within-session via an Adaptive Exploration Tree (AET) that decomposes tasks into evolving questions and reflects on intermediate findings; (2) across-sessions via dual-channel memory separating declarative knowledge (facts) from procedural knowledge (search strategies), enabling agents to learn reusable research strategies from web interaction trajectories without additional training.

Result: Consistent improvements over ReAct baselines: +25.6% on sales automation and +36.5% on legal research in low-data professional domains.

Conclusion: The web can serve as a scalable learning source for acquiring task-specific expertise in data-scarce settings, moving beyond treating it as a stateless retrieval interface.

Abstract: Real-world tasks often lack large labeled datasets, motivating extensive work on learning in low-data regimes. However, existing approaches such as few-shot prompting, instruction tuning, and synthetic data generation, continue to treat labeled or pseudo-labeled data as the primary learning signal. In contrast, human practitioners acquire expertise through repeated, self-directed interaction with the open web, progressively refining both domain knowledge and search strategies. We propose MEMENTO, a framework that treats the web as a learning signal rather than a stateless retrieval interface. MEMENTO operates at two levels: within each session, it conducts iterative web exploration via an Adaptive Exploration Tree (AET) that decomposes tasks into evolving questions and reflects on intermediate findings; across sessions, it accumulates experience through dual-channel memory, separating declarative knowledge (facts) from procedural knowledge (search strategies). This design enables agents to learn reusable research strategies and domain expertise from trajectories of web interaction without additional model training. We evaluate MEMENTO on two low-data professional domains: sales automation and legal research. Our empirical results show consistent improvements in performance over ReAct based baselines (+25.6% on sales automation and 36.5% on legal research), demonstrating that the web can serve as a scalable learning source for acquiring task-specific expertise in data-scarce settings.

</details>


### [122] [SAAS: Self-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Over-Search Mitigation in Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29796)
*Yunbo Tang,Chengyi Yang,Shiyu Liu,Zhishang Xiang,Zerui Chen,Qinggang Zhang,Jinsong Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAAS is an RL framework that mitigates over-search in agentic LLMs by introducing search boundary modeling, boundary-aware rewards, and stage-wise optimization to regulate search behavior without sacrificing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic search systems suffer from over-search: they lack self-awareness about their own knowledge boundaries, triggering unnecessary searches when internal knowledge suffices or failing to terminate even when enough evidence is gathered, leading to high latency and computational cost.

Method: SAAS introduces three components: (i) search boundary modeling — identifies the search boundary under the evolving policy by contrasting search-disabled and search-enabled rollouts; (ii) boundary-aware reward module — translates boundary awareness into trajectory-level penalties to suppress unnecessary/redundant searches; (iii) stage-wise optimization strategy — uses a sequential curriculum to prioritize reasoning over search regularization, preventing reward hacking.

Result: Extensive experiments show SAAS substantially reduces over-search while maintaining accuracy.

Conclusion: SAAS effectively cultivates dynamic self-awareness in LLM agents to precisely regulate search behavior, addressing the practical over-search limitation without compromising answer accuracy.

Abstract: Agentic search enables LLMs to solve complex multi-hop questions through iterative reasoning and external search. Despite the effectiveness, these systems often suffer from a critical limitation in practice: agents fail to recognize their own knowledge boundaries, blindly triggering searches when internal knowledge suffices and failing to terminate search even when adequate evidence has been collected. The lack of self-awareness leads to severe \textbf{over-search}, incurring substantial inference latency and prohibitive computational cost. To this end, we propose SAAS, a novel RL framework designed to cultivate dynamic self-awareness that precisely regulates search behavior without compromising accuracy. SAAS introduces three key components: (i) a search boundary modeling mechanism, which identifies the search boundary under the evolving policy by contrasting search-disabled and search-enabled rollouts; (ii) a boundary-aware reward module, which translates this boundary awareness into trajectory-level penalties, suppressing unnecessary and redundant searches; and (iii) a stage-wise optimization strategy, which leverages a sequential curriculum to prioritize reasoning over search regularization, thereby avoiding reward hacking. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SAAS substantially reduces over-search, while maintaining accuracy. Our code is anonymously released at https://github.com/XMUDeepLIT/SAAS.

</details>


### [123] [AgentDoG 1.5: A Lightweight and Scalable Alignment Framework for AI Agent Safety and Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29801)
*Dongrui Liu,Yu Li,Zhonghao Yang,Peng Wang,Guanxu Chen,Yuejin Xie,Qinghua Mao,Wanying Qu,Yanxu Zhu,Tianyi Zhou,Leitao Yuan,Zhijie Zheng,Qihao Lin,Yimin Wang,Haoyu Luo,Shuai Shao,Chen Qian,Qingyu Liu,Ling Tang,Ruiyang Qin,Qihan Ren,Junxiao Yang,Kun Wang,Zhiheng Xi,Linfeng Zhang,Ranjie Duan,Bo Zhang,Wenjie Wang,Wen Shen,Qiaosheng Zhang,Yan Teng,Chaochao Lu,Rui Mei,Man Li,Jialing Tao,Xi Lin,Tianhang Zheng,Yong Liu,Quanshi Zhang,Lei Zhu,Xingjun Ma,Junhua Liu,Hui Xue,Xiaoxiang Zuo,Xiangnan He,Chao Shen,Xianglong Liu,Minlie Huang,Jing Shao,Xia Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes AgentDoG 1.5, a lightweight and scalable agent safety alignment framework with influence-function purification, achieving state-of-the-art safety performance using only ~1k samples and reducing Docker-level deployment overhead by two orders of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern open-world agents (e.g., OpenClaw) and frontier AI models introduce new safety risks and lower attack barriers, rendering current agent alignment frameworks inadequate for real-world deployment.

Method: Updates agent safety taxonomy for emergent risks; builds a taxonomy-guided data engine with influence-function purification; trains AgentDoG 1.5 variants (0.8B–8B) on ~1k samples; constructs efficient safety SFT and RL training environment reducing Docker-level overhead by ~100x; deploys as training-free online guardrail.

Result: AgentDoG 1.5 achieves state-of-the-art performance in diverse interactive agentic scenarios, comparable to leading closed-source models like GPT-5.4, with significantly lower computational cost.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a lightweight, scalable, and effective solution for agent safety alignment, with all models and datasets openly released.

Abstract: Modern open-world agents such as OpenClaw exhibit powerful cross-environment execution capabilities yet introduce broad new safety risk sources. Meanwhile, advanced frontier AI models drastically lower attack barriers, rendering current agent alignment frameworks inadequate for real-world deployment. To tackle these emerging threats, we propose a lightweight and scalable agent safety alignment framework. Specifically, we update the agent safety taxonomy to accommodate emergent risks from Codex and OpenClaw execution scenarios. We further build a taxonomy-guided data engine with influence-function purification to train lightweight AgentDoG 1.5 variants (0.8B, 2B, 4B, and 8B parameters) using only around 1k samples, achieving comparable performance with leading closed-source models (e.g., GPT-5.4). Based on AgentDoG 1.5, we construct a highly efficient agentic safety SFT and RL training environment, which reduces deployment overhead in Docker-level environments by two orders of magnitude. Finally, we deploy AgentDoG 1.5 as a training-free online guardrail for real-time safety moderation. Extensive experimental results indicate that AgentDoG 1.5 achieves state-of-the-art performance in diverse and complex interactive agentic scenarios. All models and datasets are openly released.

</details>


### [124] [PRAIB: Peer Review AI Benchmark of Behaviour of LLM-Assisted Reviewing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29815)
*Krzysztof Żurawicki,Julia Farganus,Arkadiusz Gaweł,Mateusz Bystroński,Tomasz Jan Kajdanowicz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PRAIB, a benchmark to evaluate whether LLMs review scientific papers like humans do. Analyzing 11,000 reviews from 5 models across 1,000 ICLR/NeurIPS papers (2021-2025), they find LLM reviews diverge significantly: ratings are less variable, positively biased, overconfident, and model-dependent in cross-referencing patterns. LLMs generate longer, more complex reviews but miss atomic weaknesses human reviewers identify.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to support peer review, but it remains unknown whether they engage with manuscripts like human reviewers or merely produce review-looking text. The authors aim to systematically characterize behavioral divergences between LLM-generated and human reviews.

Method: The authors propose PRAIB (Peer Review AI Benchmark), a framework with metrics for review specificity, style, and engagement behavior. They conduct a large-scale empirical study using 11,000 reviews from 5 proprietary and open-source LLMs for 1,000 ICLR and NeurIPS papers (2021-2025), comparing machine-generated reviews against original human feedback across diverse prompting strategies.

Result: LLM ratings are less variable, positively biased, and overconfident; cross-reference patterns are model-dependent and distinct from human norms. LLMs generate longer, more complex reviews but frequently overlook atomic weaknesses identified by human reviewers.

Conclusion: PRAIB provides a diagnostic tool for identifying which aspects of the review process LLMs can reliably support today and which require further development before deployment, characterizing where and how LLM reviewing behavior departs from human norms.

Abstract: The growing number of submitted papers has motivated the exploration of Large Language Models (LLMs) as a means to support and augment the peer review process, particularly in terms of improving its speed and scalability. Yet, it remains unknown whether LLMs engage with scientific manuscripts in the same manner as human reviewers, or whether they merely produce review-looking text. To address this, we introduce the Peer Review AI Benchmark (PRAIB), a novel framework comprising thoroughly defined metrics that measure review specificity, style, and behavior of engagement. To complement the PRAIB framework, we conduct a large-scale empirical study leveraging a dataset of 11,000 reviews generated by five proprietary and open-source models for 1,000 ICLR and NeurIPS papers. Spanning the 2021--2025 period, these machine-generated reviews are compared against original human feedback across diverse prompting strategies to identify systematic behavioral divergences. Our analysis reveals that the generated reviews diverge significantly from feedback provided by human reviewers: LLM ratings are less variable, positively biased, and overconfident, and their cross-reference patterns are model-dependent and distinct from human norms. Furthermore, when evaluated through PRAIB, we observe that LLMs tend to generate longer, more complex reviews, yet frequently overlook the atomic weaknesses noted by human reviewers. By characterizing where and how LLMs reviewing behavior departs from human norms, PRAIB provides the community with a diagnostic tool for identifying which aspects of the review process LLMs can reliably support today and which require further development before deployment.

</details>


### [125] [Harnessing non-adversarial robustness in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29816)
*Qinghua Zhou,Ellina Aleshina,Andrey Lovyagin,Oleg Somov,Mikhail Seleznyov,Alexander Panchenko,Ivan Oseledets,Elena Tutubalina,Ivan Y. Tyukin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a debiasing-based fine-tuning method to make LLMs robust to semantically equivalent but textually different prompts, without full model retraining. The authors identify perturbation-induced bias as a key factor affecting robustness and show both theoretically and experimentally that debiasing can efficiently enhance and certify robustness against random prompt perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are vulnerable to semantically-neutral prompt variations (e.g., paraphrasing, wording changes), which can significantly degrade task performance. Full model retraining is expensive, so the authors seek a lightweight solution to improve robustness without retraining the entire model.

Method: The authors first theoretically analyze robustness, identifying a systematic perturbation-induced bias in neural network module outputs as a key factor. They then propose a simple fine-tuning process called "debiasing for robustness" and derive conditions under which debiasing is effective. Both theoretical and empirical validation are provided.

Result: Debiasing for robustness can effectively enhance LLM robustness to semantically-neutral prompt perturbations, and can provide certification against such perturbations. The method is quick and efficient compared to full retraining, with identified boundary conditions for when it works.

Conclusion: Debiasing is a practical, lightweight fine-tuning strategy for improving LLM robustness to prompt variations. It offers both performance gains and certifiable guarantees against random perturbations, without the computational burden of full model retraining.

Abstract: The work presents an approach for addressing the challenge of robustness in Large Language Models (LLMs) to alterations and potential errors caused by semantically similar but textually different prompts. Recent works have shown that these kinds of prompt variations can significantly impact the performance of LLMs on tasks. The central question is: can LLMs' robustness to semantically-neutral prompt alterations be acquired without expensive retraining of the entire model? We address this question both theoretically and through experiments. Our theoretical analysis reveals a crucial factor impacting model robustness - a systematic expected shift or perturbation-induced bias in neural network module outputs. Motivated by this analysis, we show that robustness can be achieved via a simple fine-tuning process: debiasing for robustness. We identify conditions when debiasing helps and when it does not, and demonstrate, through both theory and extensive experiments, that debiasing for robustness may indeed be a quick and efficient tool to enhance robustness and provide certification against random prompt perturbations.

</details>


### [126] [Quantifying and Optimizing Simplicity via Polynomial Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29823)
*Tianren Zhang,Xiangxin Li,Minghao Xiao,Guanyu Chen,Feng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces polynomial representations as a distribution-aware, low-dimensional surrogate for neural functions, using orthogonal polynomial bases along data-dependent interpolation paths. The effective degree of this representation serves as a simplicity metric predictive of generalization, outperforming sharpness-based proxies, and yields a differentiable regularizer that improves generalization across image/text classification, fine-tuning vision-language models, and reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep networks exhibit a simplicity bias that is believed to be key for generalization, but there is no broadly applicable, quantitative measure of simplicity.

Method: Polynomial representations approximate a network's predictive behavior along data-dependent interpolation paths using orthogonal polynomial bases, producing a compact functional representation whose effective degree serves as a simplicity metric.

Result: The effective degree metric consistently outperforms existing generalization proxies like sharpness across tasks and architectures. The differentiable simplicity regularizer derived from this representation consistently improves generalization in image classification, text classification, fine-tuning contrastive vision-language models, and reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: Polynomial representations provide a practical, distribution-aware simplicity measure that both predicts and improves generalization across diverse domains and architectures.

Abstract: Deep networks often exhibit a preference for "simple" solutions, and such a simplicity bias is widely believed to play a key role in generalization. Yet a broadly applicable, quantitative measure of simplicity remains elusive. We introduce polynomial representations as a distribution-aware, low-dimensional surrogate for neural functions: we approximate a network's predictive behavior along data-dependent interpolation paths using orthogonal polynomial bases, yielding a compact functional representation. We show that the effective degree of this representation serves as a practical simplicity metric that is predictive of generalization across tasks and architectures, and consistently outperforms existing generalization proxies such as sharpness. Finally, polynomial representations naturally yield a differentiable simplicity regularizer, which consistently improves generalization in image and text classification, fine-tuning contrastive vision-language models, and reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [127] [OptSkills: Learning Generalizable Optimization Skills from Problem Archetypes via Cluster-Based Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29829)
*Haochen Yang,Ke Zhao,Mengyuan Ma,Xingyu Lu,Xiangfeng Wang,Hong Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OptSkills is an LLM-based agent system that learns archetype-centric workflow-level skills for robust optimization problem solving, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy (68.27% micro-averaged) and strong out-of-distribution generalization (72.79% on OOD NLCO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based automated optimization methods suffer from limited generalization: they are sensitive to superficial narrative variations, reuse experience only at the case level, and fail to adapt to shifted or emerging problem types.

Method: OptSkills clusters problems by underlying archetypes (not surface narratives), explores diverse modeling paradigms and solver configurations within each cluster, distills successful trajectories into reusable workflow-level skills, and refines/expands the skill library via newly obtained trajectories for out-of-distribution adaptation.

Result: 68.27% micro-averaged accuracy on diverse datasets; 26.91% on MIPLIB-NL (4.53% better than DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking); 72.79% on OOD NLCO benchmark after skill learning on Nano-CO.

Conclusion: Archetype-centric skill learning significantly improves both in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization for automated optimization, outperforming strong baselines.

Abstract: Leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically formulate and solve optimization problems from natural language has emerged as an efficient paradigm for automated optimization. However, existing methods still exhibit limited generalization: they are sensitive to superficial narrative variations, reuse experience mainly at the case level, and struggle to adapt to shifted or emerging problem types. We propose OptSkills, an archetype-centric skill learning and reasoning agent system for optimization modeling and solving. To improve robust generalization, our system clusters problems by their underlying archetypes rather than surface narratives. To improve in-distribution generalization, it explores diverse modeling paradigms and solver configurations within each cluster, then distills successful trajectories into reusable workflow-level skills. To improve out-of-distribution generalization, it refines existing skills or expands the skill library using newly obtained trajectories. Our system achieves a state-of-the-art micro-averaged accuracy of 68.27% on datasets encompassing diverse problem types and scenarios. In addition, on MIPLIB-NL, a highly challenging large-scale and high-dimensional benchmark, it achieves 26.91% accuracy, outperforming DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking by 4.53%. After skill learning on Nano-CO, it reaches 72.79% on the OOD NLCO benchmark. Code and skills are available at https://github.com/fujiwaranoM0kou/OptSkills.

</details>


### [128] [OmniMatBench: A Human-Calibrated Multimodal Reasoning Benchmark Across 19 Materials Science Subfields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29833)
*Wanhao Liu,Jiaqing Xie,Qian Tan,Weida Wang,Jue Wang,Ran Sun,Zhuo Yang,Wanli Ouyang,Lei Bai,Tianfan Fu,Lu Chen,Xin Chen,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniMatBench is a human-calibrated multimodal reasoning benchmark for materials science containing 3,171 expert-curated problems across 19 subfields. The best MLLM achieves only 0.372 overall score, revealing substantial gaps in materials-science reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing materials benchmarks focus narrowly on property prediction, knowledge QA, or characterization understanding, but neglect the broader reasoning process from materials knowledge to application. The authors aim to fill this gap with a comprehensive benchmark.

Method: The authors curated 3,171 expert-verified QA and calculation problems spanning 19 materials-science subfields across four major categories: fundamental knowledge, structural/engineering materials, processing/manufacturing, and functional/applied materials. They evaluated 13 open-source and closed-source MLLMs.

Result: Best model achieves only 0.372 overall score; strong variation across subfields; models exhibit fixed reasoning heuristics, uneven materials knowledge, and limited high-level knowledge application even with formula, retrieval, and code assistance.

Conclusion: OmniMatBench reveals substantial reasoning gaps in current MLLMs for materials science and provides a foundation for developing reliable AI assistants in this domain.

Abstract: As multimodal language models play an increasingly important role in scientific research, materials science offers a critical testbed due to its interdisciplinary, multimodal, and application-driven nature. However, existing materials benchmarks mainly focus on property prediction, knowledge QA, or characterization understanding, leaving the broader reasoning process from materials knowledge to application underexplored. To fill this gap, we present OmniMatBench, a human-calibrated multimodal reasoning benchmark for materials science. OmniMatBench contains 3,171 expert-curated QA and calculation problems across 19 materials-science subfields, spanning fundamental materials knowledge, structural and engineering materials, materials processing and manufacturing, and functional and applied materials. We evaluate 13 open-source and closed-source MLLMs and find that the best model achieves only a 0.372 overall score, revealing a substantial gap in current materials-science reasoning.
